"title": "Immunization and Prevention Measures Listed in the Communicable Disease Handout",
"body": "## What it is\nEvery preventive vaccine, immune globulin and control step the handout names, in one place.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Chickenpox**: vaccine; VZIG within 10 days of exposure. Exclude the child from school for 1 week after the rash first appears.\n- **Measles**: anti-measles vaccine at 9 months (single dose); MMR at 15 months, second dose at 11 to 12 years. Do not give to pregnant women, or to people with active TB, leukemia, lymphoma or weakened immunity.\n- **Rubella**: live attenuated vaccine (MMR); pregnant women must avoid exposure; immune serum globulin one week after exposure.\n- **Herpes zoster**: varicella vaccination; avoid contact with varicella cases.\n- **Diphtheria and pertussis**: three doses of DPT for infants and children; reinforcing (booster) dose for previously immunized children; pasteurize milk; report cases.\n- **Influenza**: yearly vaccine, avoid crowds and shared towels, glasses and utensils.\n- **Pneumonia**: pneumonia vaccine, prevent colds and influenza, avoid cold, pollution, fatigue, alcohol.\n- **Tuberculosis and leprosy**: BCG for babies; avoid overcrowding; test exposed persons.\n- **Anthrax**: animal vaccination, supervised disposal of dead animals, vaccine for at-risk workers.\n- **Mumps**: MMR, report cases, isolate.\n- **Polio**: oral polio vaccine; boosters every 10 years for travel to endemic areas (as the handout states).\n- **Tetanus**: toxoid booster every 10 years, wound care; tetanus immune globulin after dirty wounds.\n- **Rabies**: vaccinate dogs at 3 months of age and yearly; confine a dog that bit a person for 10 to 14 days.\n- **Cholera, typhoid, dysentery, amoebiasis**: safe water (boiled, chlorinated), pasteurized milk, sanitary excreta disposal, food-handler supervision, fly control, handwashing.\n- **Malaria, dengue, filariasis**: mosquito nets, house spraying, repellents, clearing breeding sites; chloroquine prophylaxis for malaria travelers.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLive vaccines (measles, MMR, rubella) are avoided in pregnancy and in the immunocompromised. Questions pair a disease with the correct vaccine age or ask which control step breaks the chain of infection.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Types of Immunity and Antigens]], [[Chain of Infection and Modes of Transmission]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVaccine ages and boosters (especially measles, MMR, polio) change with national programs; use the current DOH schedule rather than the handout's figures.",

"title": "Vectors, Intermediate Hosts, Reservoirs and Sources by Disease",
"body": "## What it is\nA \"who carries it, where does it come from\" list. Exam items often ask for the vector or the intermediate host.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Aedes aegypti** (female) bites carry dengue (and chikungunya virus is listed with it).\n- **Anopheles** (female) carries malaria; also by blood transfusion and across the placenta.\n- **Aedes poecilius** is the filariasis mosquito in the handout (worms: Wuchereria bancrofti, Brugia malayi, Brugia timori, Loa loa).\n- **Oncomelania hupensis quadrasi** snail is the intermediate host of Schistosoma japonicum.\n- **Snails and crabs**: paragonimiasis comes from eating raw crab or crayfish; reservoir hosts are cats, dogs, rats, pigs.\n- **Rats and other animals**: leptospirosis from urine in water or food, entering through broken skin.\n- **Dogs and other warm-blooded mammals**: rabies in saliva.\n- **Birds and sometimes pigs**: avian influenza.\n- **Cattle, sheep, goats, horses, pigs and their hair, wool or bone meal**: anthrax.\n- **Soil and feces**: tetanus spores. **Soil, canned or smoked foods, honey in infants**: botulism.\n- **Humans as the only reservoir**: diphtheria (and milk can be a vehicle).\n- **Flies**: carry dysentery and amoebiasis organisms to food.\n- **Carriers**: typhoid, cholera and amoebiasis carriers pass organisms without being ill.\n- **Human skin and fomites**: scabies; hair, clothing seams: lice.\n- **Contaminated water**: cholera, typhoid, schistosomiasis, leptospirosis.\n\n## How to reason about it\nCause -> control: if the link is a vector, break it with nets, spraying, repellents and clearing breeding sites; if it is water or food, use boiling, chlorination and sanitation; if it is an animal reservoir, vaccinate or control the animal.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chain of Infection and Modes of Transmission]], [[Dengue Fever]], [[Malaria]], [[Schistosomiasis]], [[Leptospirosis]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nFilariasis vector species and the mention of Loa loa as an \"African eye worm\" in the handout are unusual; confirm which organisms and mosquito belong to Philippine filariasis.",

"title": "Gut (Fecal-Oral) Infections Compared",
"body": "## What it is\nCholera, typhoid, bacillary dysentery, amoebiasis, and botulism are grouped because they arrive through food, water or hands. Telling them apart is a common exam task.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Cholera**: Vibrio El Tor; rice-water stool, vomiting, washerwoman's hands, oliguria; shock from fluid loss. Fastest incubation (6 to 48 hours).\n- **Typhoid**: Salmonella typhi; stepwise fever, rose spots, Peyer's patch ulceration; risks are intestinal hemorrhage and perforation.\n- **Bacillary dysentery**: Shigella species; tenesmus, bloody-mucoid stools, cramps, fever; anti-diarrheals are contraindicated.\n- **Amoebiasis**: Entamoeba histolytica; cyst is the infective stage, trophozoite invades tissue; foul stools with blood-streaked mucus, right iliac tenderness; metronidazole.\n- **Botulism**: preformed toxin; descending paralysis, not diarrhea, is the main picture.\n- Enteric isolation applies to cholera, typhoid and amoebiasis; handwashing and excreta disposal break the chain for all.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Replace fluids first in cholera and severe dysentery (circulation/ABC: hypovolemia kills).\n2. Watch for bleeding and sudden right-lower abdominal pain or rigid abdomen in typhoid (perforation; notify the physician at once).\n3. Accurate intake and output and vital signs.\n4. Enteric precautions, handwashing, safe disposal of stool.\n5. Teach safe water and food, and fly control.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStool picture is the clue: rice-water = cholera; bloody mucus with tenesmus = shigella; foul with blood-streaked mucus = amoeba; stepwise fever with rose spots = typhoid.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cholera]], [[Typhoid Fever]], [[Bacillary Dysentery (Shigellosis)]], [[Amoebiasis]], [[Botulism]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nOral rehydration versus IV choice depends on dehydration severity; verify current protocols.",

"body": "## What it is\nThree mosquito-borne diseases in the handout, compared so their vectors, signs and controls are not mixed up.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Dengue**: virus types 1 to 4; female Aedes aegypti; incubation 3 to 15 days; three stages (febrile, toxic/hemorrhagic, convalescent); platelets fall and hemoconcentration rises at least 20%; tourniquet test; NS1 confirmatory; no aspirin.\n- **Malaria**: four Plasmodium species (falciparum the most common in the handout); female Anopheles; paroxysms of chills, fever, drenching sweat; splenomegaly; chloroquine prophylaxis weekly.\n- **Filariasis**: Wuchereria and Brugia worms; incubation 8 to 16 months; asymptomatic, acute (lymphadenitis, lymphangitis, orchitis), chronic (hydrocele, lymphedema, elephantiasis); nocturnal blood test; diethylcarbamazine.\n- Shared control: nets (insecticide-treated), repellents, house spraying, remove stagnant water, clothing covering arms and legs, avoid outdoor night activity.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Dengue: watch for shock and bleeding (falling temperature with severe abdominal pain, melena, hematemesis); replace fluids fast; ice bag on forehead and nose bridge for nosebleed.\n2. Malaria: keep the room mosquito-free; manage chills, fever and sweating comfort; give prophylaxis as ordered.\n3. Filariasis: support, hygiene, and psychological care for disfigurement; yearly medicine in endemic areas.\n4. Teach prevention, report cases.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n\"Febrile drop with bleeding\" = dengue danger period, not recovery. Night-time blood sample = filariasis. Weekly chloroquine before entering an endemic area = malaria.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Dengue Fever]], [[Malaria]], [[Filariasis]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHandout says temperature \"lowers\" in the toxic stage; this drop is the critical phase when shock risk is highest.",

"title": "Rash and Skin Illnesses Compared",
"body": "## What it is\nChickenpox, measles, rubella, herpes zoster, scabies and pediculosis all affect the skin but differ in cause, rash pattern and isolation. This note puts the differences side by side.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- **Chickenpox**: macules/papules for hours, vesicles 3 to 4 days, then granular scabs; all stages at once; airborne and contact precautions; lasting immunity.\n- **Measles**: Koplik spots then maculopapular rash starting after fever and cough; airborne precautions, negative-pressure room; complications bronchopneumonia, otitis media, encephalitis.\n- **Rubella**: mild; postauricular and suboccipital nodes first; rash 1 to 5 days; danger is to the fetus (cataract, deafness, heart defects, small head).\n- **Herpes zoster**: reactivation of the chickenpox virus along one nerve; painful, one side only; nursing: avoid scratching, analgesics, acyclovir.\n- **Scabies**: mite; itch worse at night; burrows in finger webs, wrists, elbows; treat the whole household.\n- **Pediculosis**: lice; itch; nits behind the ears and nape; permethrin first; wash linens in hot water.\n- **Leprosy**: skin patches with loss of sensation; protect anesthetic areas.\n\n## Priority nursing actions\n1. Identify the precaution type (airborne, droplet, contact) before entering the room.\n2. Protect from complications: airway in measles, eyes in leprosy.\n3. Relieve itch and prevent scratching, trim nails.\n4. Protect pregnant women from rubella exposure.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nRash that is vesicular in several stages at once = chickenpox. Rash after nodes behind the ears = rubella. Cough, coryza, conjunctivitis then Koplik spots = measles. Pregnant client exposed = rubella is the hazard.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Chickenpox (Varicella)]], [[Measles (Rubeola)]], [[German Measles (Rubella)]], [[Herpes Zoster (Shingles)]], [[Scabies]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nIsolation level for chickenpox is called \"strict isolation\" in the handout; modern practice is airborne plus contact precautions.",

{
"id": "np1-chn-basics",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community Health Nursing Basics",
"body": "## What it is\nCommunity health nursing (CHN) is nursing practice that combines public health and nursing skills to protect and promote the health of a whole population, not only of the sick person in front of you. The community is the ultimate client; individuals and families are the entry points to reach it. The usual textbook definition of public health comes from the public health pioneer C.E.A. Winslow: the science and art of preventing disease, prolonging life and promoting health and efficiency through organized community effort. The focus is prevention and health promotion first, cure second.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Clients of CHN, from smallest to largest unit: the individual, the family (the basic unit of care and the unit of service), population groups (people sharing a risk, age or condition such as mothers, school children, the elderly), and the community. The handout lists four levels of clientele: individual, family, population, community.\n- Defining statements that are often quoted: the American Nurses Association (1980) calls CHN a synthesis of nursing practice and public health practice applied to promoting and preserving the health of populations. The Association of Community Health Nursing Educators (1990) stresses that the focus is the community as a whole, with care of individuals, families and groups given within the context of the community's health. Ruth B. Freeman describes it as a service by a professional nurse to communities, groups, families and individuals at home, in health centers, clinics, schools and workplaces, for promotion of health, prevention of illness, and care of the sick at home with rehabilitation.\n- Scope: preventive, promotive, curative and rehabilitative care, with preventive and promotive emphasis. Care is continuous, not episodic.\n- Practice settings: home, health center or rural health unit (RHU), clinic, school, workplace. Barangay health workers and midwives work alongside the public health nurse.\n- Roles commonly listed: care provider, educator, advocate, manager and supervisor, coordinator and collaborator, researcher, referral agent, change agent, and facilitator of community participation (see the roles note for the Philippine list).\n- Subspecialties named in the handout: school nursing, occupational health nursing, community mental health nursing, and public health nursing.\n- Community-based nursing is different in emphasis: it is a philosophy of care in which clients and their families are cared for as they move among services outside hospitals, while public health nursing combines nursing, public health and some social assistance, working for the whole population.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the community and its families first (community diagnosis) because services must match real needs.\n2. Plan with the community, not for it, so that people own the plan.\n3. Deliver care at the lowest effective level, using home visits, clinics and group teaching.\n4. Refer when the need exceeds the level of the RHU, and follow up.\n5. Evaluate outcomes using health indicators and community feedback.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a question that describes a whole barangay problem (for example repeated diarrhea cases from one water source) is a community-level problem, so the best action targets the community (water, sanitation, teaching groups), not just treating the one child in front of you.\n- Order of preference in answers: prevention and promotion beat cure; the community beats the individual as the target; working with the people beats working for them. The reason is that prevention reaches more people at lower cost and CHN resources are limited.\n- If an answer choice treats the individual only and another changes the cause for many, the second is usually the better CHN answer, unless the stem describes an emergency (then stabilize first, safety before program).\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Health means wellness, not just absence of disease. Prevention is the main strategy.\n- Public health nursing is often treated as a synonym of community health nursing; some books separate them by employer (government versus private). The handout defines the public health nurse as a nurse in local or national health departments or public schools, including the school nurse.\n- Rate this order when asked about the unit of service: family is the unit of service; the community is the ultimate client.\n\n## People behind CHN\n- Florence Nightingale: founder of modern nursing; promoted sanitation, statistics and trained nurses, and supported district nursing for the sick poor at home.\n- William Rathbone: businessman who funded district nursing in Liverpool, England, after his wife was nursed at home.\n- Lillian Wald: American nurse who coined the term public health nurse and founded the Henry Street Settlement in New York; a model of nursing outreach to poor communities and schools.\n- Mary Breckinridge: founded the Frontier Nursing Service in the United States (nurse-midwives on horseback).\n- Clara Barton: associated with the American Red Cross.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nLook for the question that asks who the client is, what the focus is, or which person is matched to which contribution. A trap: choosing the individual as the ultimate client; the best answer is the community (family if the stem says unit of service). Another trap: treating community-based nursing (care outside hospitals as clients move between services) as the same as public health nursing (population-focused).\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: W-W-N = Wald (public health nurse, Henry Street), Winslow (definition), Nightingale (district nursing roots).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Community Organizing (COPAR) Phases]], [[Family Nursing Process: Assessment]], [[Public Health Nurse Roles and Qualifications]], [[Community-Based Nursing, Public Health Nursing and CHN Subspecialties]], [[Community Health Nursing Process: Assessment to Evaluation]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact list and wording of CHN roles differ between books.\n- People behind CHN: some Philippine textbooks add local pioneers; verify local names and dates in your own reviewer before memorizing.\n- The handout attributes the public health definition to Winslow without initials, and the statements by ANA 1980 and ACHNE 1990 are quoted from the handout; confirm wording and years in your textbook.\n",

"CHN",
"definition",
"roles",
"clients",
"history"
],
"priority": "high",
"outlineRefs": [
"Community Health Nursing",
"CHN, PHC",
"Clients of CHN",
"People Behind CHN"
],

"id": "np1-copar-principles",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community Organizing (COPAR) Basic Principles",
"body": "## What it is\nCommunity Organizing Participatory Action Research (COPAR) is a way of working with a community in which the people themselves identify problems, plan, act and evaluate, with the nurse as a facilitator. It is a continuing, educational process aimed at empowerment: helping people gain the ability and confidence to solve their own health problems and sustain solutions after the nurse leaves. The handout describes it as the strategy used in a Health Resource Development Program (HRDP III) to deliver primary health care in depressed and underserved communities so that they become self-reliant, and defines it as a collective, participatory, transformative, sustained and systematic process of building people's organizations by mobilizing and strengthening their capabilities and resources to resolve their own issues.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The three basic principles in the handout: (1) people, especially the most oppressed, exploited and deprived sectors, are open to change, can change and can bring about change; (2) the work is based on the interests of the poorest sectors of society; (3) it should lead to a self-reliant community and society.\n- The people are the center. The nurse is a catalyst, facilitator and educator, not the owner of the project.\n- The process is participatory: action and research happen together. The community collects information, reflects on it and acts.\n- Methods and character: a progressive cycle of action, reflection, action that starts from small, local, concrete issues the people identify; consciousness raising, where learning grows out of concrete action and enriches the next action; participatory and mass-based (biased in favor of the poor and powerless); group-oriented, not leader-oriented, so leaders emerge and are tested through action rather than appointed by outsiders.\n- Emphasis in community organizing: members of the community solve their own problems; direction is internal, not external; developing the capacity to set up a project matters more than the project itself; people become aware of how health care delivery fits into society as a whole.\n- Objectives of community organizing: make people aware of social realities so local initiative grows and local resources are used; form structures that defend people's basic interests; start responsible actions that deal with health and social problems together.\n- Importance: a tool for community development and empowerment, prepares people to take over management of development programs, and increases participation.\n- Start where the people are: their perceived needs, culture, strengths and resources come before the nurse's own agenda. Work with community leaders and respect existing structures (barangay council, people's organizations, church and school groups). Involve all sectors, including vulnerable groups.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Learn the community first (integration) because trust must exist before any action.\n2. Help people recognize their own problems; if the nurse names the problem alone, ownership is lost.\n3. Develop leaders and a core group so work continues.\n4. Support, advise and step back gradually; the end point is phase-out.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a project that stops when the nurse leaves shows low ownership; the fix is to build the capacity and leadership of the people, not to bring more outside resources.\n- Order of preference: community decides over nurse decides; small local issues first over big ambitious projects (the action-reflection-action cycle begins small); capacity over project; internal direction over external direction. The reason is sustainability: only what the people own survives phase-out.\n- Safety and assessment still come first in a real emergency, but in a question that names COPAR, choose the answer that returns decisions to the community.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nTeach in a way that builds skills (how to hold a meeting, how to monitor a project) rather than only facts. Use local language and examples.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nTypical stems describe a nurse doing something for the community (deciding the project, funding it, running it herself). The correct choice is the one that moves decisions to the community. Words like facilitator, empowerment, sustainable, participation and self-reliant signal the right answer. A trap: acting fast because the problem is urgent in your view, when the question asks about COPAR philosophy. Another trap: picking the answer in which the nurse appoints the leaders; the handout says leaders emerge through action.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: PEOPLE decide, nurse FACILITATES, project SURVIVES without her.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Community Organizing (COPAR) Phases]], [[COPAR Ten Critical Activities and Participatory Action Research]], [[Primary Health Care Pillars and Characteristics]], [[Community Health Nursing Basics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some books list five to seven numbered principles with different wording. Match your reviewer's list for recall questions, but the logic (people-centered, empowering, sustainable) is the same.\n- The handout names the program as HRDP III and cites a 1994 National Rural Community Organizing conference for the definition; confirm the program name and source in your textbook.\n",

"id": "np1-copar-phases",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community Organizing (COPAR) Phases",
"body": "## What it is\nCOPAR proceeds through ordered phases, from preparing and choosing a site to a community that runs its own program. Know the order because questions often ask which phase comes next or which activity belongs to which phase.\n\n## Must-know facts\nThe handout teaches six phases. Learn this sequence first:\n1. Pre-entry phase (done before going to the community): community consultations about site selection, criteria for choosing the site, site selection itself, preliminary social investigation (PSI), and networking with local government units, NGOs and other departments. Site criteria: socio-economically depressed and underserved with most residents poor; health services hard to reach; poor health status; relative peace and order; acceptance of the program by the community. PSI uses secondary data from government offices (such as the provincial health office and RHU) and other community programs, coordination with GO and NGO extension workers, and ocular observation of accessibility, terrain, settlement patterns and physical resources.\n2. Entry phase: integration with the residents, deepening social investigation, information dissemination, and formation of the core group (CG). Core group member criteria: respected in the community, from the poor sector, responsible and committed to social change, willing to learn, able to communicate. The core group's work includes delivery of basic health services, dialogue with other community organizations, and Self-Awareness and Leadership Training (SALT).\n3. Community study, diagnosis or research phase: choose the research team, train them in data collection, plan the gathering, gather data, validate it with the community, present the community diagnosis with recommendations, and prioritize needs and problems for action.\n4. Community organization and capacity-building phase: community meetings, election and induction of community health organization (CHO) officers, management systems and procedures, team building and action-reflection-action sessions (ARAS), working committees, and training of officers and leaders.\n5. Community action phase: select and train community health workers (CHWs), set up linkages, networks and referral systems, project implementation, monitoring and evaluation (PIME) of health and development projects, and start resource-mobilization schemes.\n6. Sustenance and strengthening phase: constitution and by-laws, developing secondary leaders, formalizing linkages and referral, a financing scheme for the community health program, viable committees and systems, continuing education of leaders and members, and medium and long-term plans.\n\nA commonly taught longer sequence in other books (pre-entry, entry, social preparation/integration, core group formation, organization, education and training, mobilization, ground work and evaluation, phase-out) fits inside the six-phase outline: the nurse withdraws gradually as the sixth phase matures.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Prepare before entering: choose a site by criteria and gather secondary data; this prevents wasting effort on a place that is not ready or not accepting.\n2. Seek formal permission and stay to integrate; skipping the entry step damages trust.\n3. Facilitate community diagnosis so that the people see the data and set priorities themselves.\n4. Develop leaders and organize before starting big projects.\n5. Train community health workers and run projects with monitoring and evaluation.\n6. Phase out only when the community shows it can plan and act without help.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if residents cannot yet express needs, the stage is entry or integration; if they have data and no organization, the stage is community diagnosis moving to organization; if officers are elected and committees work, the stage is action.\n- Order of reasoning: relationships before data, data before organization, organization before projects, projects before phase-out. The reason is that each step needs the trust and capacity created by the one before it.\n- Prefer the answer in which the community validates and prioritizes data over the one in which the nurse alone interprets it.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Preliminary social investigation (PSI): secondary data and observation done before entry.\n- Deepening social investigation: continuing study after entry.\n- Community validation: residents confirm the findings.\n- ARAS: action-reflection-action session used for team building.\n- PIME: project implementation, monitoring and evaluation.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give an activity (for example, the nurse reviews RHU records and walks the terrain, the group elects officers, or CHWs are trained) and ask for the phase. Anchor on verbs: secondary data and ocular visit mean pre-entry; core group and SALT mean entry; validation and prioritizing mean community diagnosis; election of officers means organization; CHW training and PIME mean action; constitution and by-laws mean sustenance. Trap: confusing core group formation (entry) with election of officers (organization phase).\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me, a story order: Prepare the site, Enter and mix, Study the place, Organize the people, Act with workers, Sustain and step back (P-E-S-O-A-S).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Community Organizing (COPAR) Basic Principles]], [[COPAR Ten Critical Activities and Participatory Action Research]], [[Community Health Nursing Basics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Books name and group the phases differently (some give five, some more, and some merge ground work with mobilization or pre-entry with entry). Memorize the grouping in the reviewer you use, and verify the sequence with your instructor (verify local policy / school version).\n- The handout puts SALT and core group formation in the entry phase, while some books place core group training later; confirm where your school places each activity.\n",

"id": "np1-doh",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Department of Health (DOH) is the national government agency that leads the Philippine health system. It sets national health policy and standards, regulates, and provides leadership in health, while delivery of basic services to the people is largely handled by local government units after devolution.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three core roles in the handout: leadership in health (national policy and regulatory institution; leads formulation, monitoring and evaluation of national policies, plans and programs; advocate for adoption of health policies); enabler and capacity builder (innovates new strategies, oversees monitoring and evaluation of national plans and programs, ensures the highest achievable standards of quality health care, promotion and protection); and administrator of specific services (manages selected national health facilities and hospitals, runs direct services for emergent concerns that need new complicated technology, and administers health emergency response).\n- Vision in the handout: Filipinos among the healthiest people in Southeast Asia by 2022 and Asia by 2040. Mission: to lead the country in developing a productive, resilient, equitable and people-centered health system. The overriding goal is health sector reform through the Health Sector Reform Agenda (HSRA).\n- Devolution (under the Local Government Code, RA 7160) moved structures, personnel and budgets from the provincial health level down to the barangays to local government units. The DOH keeps policy, standards, technical assistance, regulation and services of national scope. The levels of care in a local health system run from the barangay health station to the RHU or city health office, then district, provincial and regional hospitals, up to national referral centers.\n- Related agencies often linked to the DOH in questions include PhilHealth (social health insurance, the financing arm of reform) and attached regulators of food and drugs. Check the current list.\n- Barangay health workers (BHWs) are community volunteers trained and supervised by the RHU nurse and midwife.\n- The public health nurse in the RHU supervises midwives and BHWs, manages programs, and links the community with the municipal health officer.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Know the referral chain so that the client goes to the right level.\n2. Implement national DOH programs locally (immunization, nutrition, maternal care, communicable disease control).\n3. Report health data upward through the information system so that policy fits reality.\n4. Coordinate with local officials, who fund and administer services.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if a barangay lacks a service, ask who is responsible. Basic service delivery belongs to the LGU (municipal or city), policy and standards belong to the DOH, and hospital financing support involves PhilHealth. Route the request to the correct level rather than to the nearest office.\n- Order: national sets rules, local delivers service, nurse links the two. The reason is devolution; the DOH cannot run every health station.\n- When both an LGU answer and a DOH answer appear for a service-delivery question, prefer LGU; when the question is about national policy, standards or regulation, prefer DOH.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nQuestions ask who is responsible for what (national policy and standards versus local service delivery), where a client should be referred first, or what DOH function a scenario shows. The trap is mixing DOH national roles with local government roles. Another trap: calling the DOH the owner of RHUs; RHUs are run by municipal LGUs.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Sentrong Sigla Program]], [[Magna Carta of Public Health Workers]], [[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Health Sector Reform Agenda and FOURmula One]], [[Local Health System and Inter-Local Health Zones]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Official vision, mission, the exact number of DOH functions, the name of the current reform framework and the current organizational structure: verify local policy; these change. The 2022 and 2040 vision years come from the handout and may be outdated.\n- Which specific agencies are attached to the DOH: verify.\n- The laws and executive orders behind reorganization and devolution: verify the numbers and years in your own reviewer.\n",

"id": "np1-family-assessment",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Family Nursing Process: Assessment",
"body": "## What it is\nIn CHN, the family is the unit of service, so the nursing process (assessment, diagnosis, planning, implementation, evaluation, ADPIE) is applied to the family. Assessment is the first step and has two levels. It collects data on the family as a whole, not just the sick member.\n\n## Must-know facts\nFirst-level assessment (family structure, characteristics and general status):\n- Family structure and characteristics, including members, relationships and roles, and decision-making patterns.\n- Socioeconomic and cultural characteristics.\n- Home and environmental conditions (water, toilet, waste disposal, housing, pets, vectors).\n- Health status of each member, including immunization and nutritional status.\n- Values and practices about health and illness.\n- Use of community resources.\n\nSecond-level assessment (the family's ability to do its health tasks):\n- Looks at five basic family health tasks: recognizing a health problem, making decisions about care, caring for a sick, disabled or dependent member, maintaining a healthy home environment, and using community resources.\n- The nurse classifies each problem as a health threat (conditions that may lead to disease or injury, like poor sanitation), a health deficit (existing illness or disability, or a gap between actual and achievable health, including developmental lag), or a foreseeable crisis (expected periods of unusual demand on the family, such as childbirth or entry into school). The handout adds a fourth state, wellness potential: the likelihood that health will be maintained or improved, depending on the family's desire.\n- Assessment gives an estimate of how far the family is from the level of health possible for it, points out specific deficiencies or needed guidance, and helps predict the effect of nursing interventions.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Establish rapport and obtain consent at the first home visit; people share more when they trust you.\n2. Collect first-level data, then use it to find problems.\n3. Do second-level assessment of the five tasks to learn why the family cannot solve the problem.\n4. Validate data and record it in the family folder or family health record.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to priority action: the family task that failed is the cause and the problem classification is the finding. For example, a family that does not recognize that a child's cough is serious (failure of task 1) needs teaching first; a family that knows but cannot afford treatment (task 2 or 5) needs referral and linkage to resources.\n- Order: assess before teaching or treating, and assess the family before judging it. The reason is that a plan built on guesses wastes the limited number of visits.\n- Classification rule of thumb: disease or disability present is a deficit; risk conditions without disease are a threat; an anticipated stress point is a foreseeable crisis.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- A family nursing diagnosis states the problem and its cause, usually the family's inability to perform a task.\n- Data collection tools include interview, observation, physical exam and records review.\n- Health deficit versus health threat is a favorite trap; deficits are already present, threats are not yet disease.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nTeaching starts only after the family agrees which problem matters to them. Use their words and respect their beliefs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give a household description and ask you to classify a problem (threat, deficit or crisis) or name the family health task that failed. Trap: a family with an ill member has a deficit; a household with unsafe water but no disease yet has a threat; a mother about to deliver is a foreseeable crisis.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Planning and Prioritization]], [[Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Community Health Nursing Process: Assessment to Evaluation]], [[Home Visit and Bag Technique]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDifferent books use slightly different names for the levels and for the family tasks. Verify the wording used in your school. Some books treat wellness potential as a separate state and others fold it into the problem typology.\n",

"family",
"ADPIE",
"assessment",
"family health tasks"

"a. Assessment"

"id": "np1-family-planning",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Family Nursing Process: Planning and Prioritization",
"body": "## What it is\nPlanning in the family nursing process means deciding which family problem to tackle first, setting goals, choosing interventions and deciding who does what and when. Because a household usually has many problems and the nurse has limited time, a scoring method ranks them.\n\n## Must-know facts\nGoal setting:\n- A goal is the broad outcome (the declaration of purpose that gives direction); objectives are specific, measurable steps. Good objectives follow the SMART idea: specific, measurable, attainable, realistic, time-bound.\n- Goal setting is the first step of planning. After it come constructing a plan of action (choosing among possible courses of action, selecting types of nursing intervention, identifying resources) and developing an operational plan (setting priorities, phasing and coordinating activities). The parameters for evaluation are also set during planning.\n- The family takes part in setting goals so they accept them.\n\nPrioritizing family problems with the scale commonly taught in the Philippines (four criteria, total weight 5). Verify the exact scores in your own textbook:\n1. Nature of the problem, weight 1: health deficit scores highest (3 of 3), health threat next (2 of 3), foreseeable crisis lowest (1 of 3).\n2. Preventive potential, weight 1: high 3 of 3, moderate 2 of 3, low 1 of 3.\n3. Modifiability of the problem, weight 2: easily modifiable 2 of 2, partially 1 of 2, not modifiable 0.\n4. Salience, weight 1: family sees it as needing immediate attention 2 of 2, sees it but not urgent 1 of 2, does not perceive it as a problem 0.\n\nFormula for each criterion: (score obtained divided by the highest possible score for that criterion) multiplied by the weight. Add the four results. The highest total is the first priority.\n\nWorked example, invented for practice: a deficit (3/3 x 1 = 1), moderate preventive potential (2/3 x 1 = 0.67), partially modifiable (1/2 x 2 = 1), not urgent in the family's eyes (1/2 x 1 = 0.5). Total = 3.17.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. List the family's identified problems.\n2. Score each problem on each criterion with the family's view included (salience) so priority reflects both science and the family's perception.\n3. Compute totals and rank them.\n4. If scores tie, use clinical judgment: life-threatening or contagious conditions and children come first.\n5. Write objectives and interventions for the top-ranked problem, then the others.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: high total means the problem is real, can be changed and matters to the family, so the visits will be well spent. A low-modifiability problem (weight 2) drops down even when it is serious, because nursing effort will not change it.\n- Order for ties and safety: a life-threatening or rapidly spreading problem (for example an active infectious case in a household with infants) overrides the arithmetic; assess before you score and protect people first.\n- Check your arithmetic in the order: divide by the maximum of that criterion, multiply by its weight, add. The reason for dividing is to make every criterion comparable; the weight then shows importance.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Weights differ: modifiability counts double, so it matters most in the formula.\n- Health threats and deficits are tackled while crises are anticipated.\n- Evaluation parameters are written during planning, not left to the end.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give scores per criterion and ask for the total or the priority problem. Do the arithmetic carefully and watch the weights; the common trap is forgetting to multiply by the weight or dividing by the wrong maximum. Another trap: scoring nature of the problem with threat higher than deficit.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: the weights are 1, 1, 2, 1 in the order Nature, Preventive, Modifiability, Salience; only modifiability is doubled.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Assessment]], [[Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation]], [[Community Health Nursing Process: Assessment to Evaluation]], [[Management Functions of the Community Health Nurse]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The exact fractions and weights of the family-problem prioritization scale (verify local policy and your textbook; I am fairly but not fully certain of the values above; the handout itself does not give the scale).\n- Some schools use fewer or more criteria, so copy your school's version.\n",

"goal setting",
"scoring",
"priority"

"3. Scoring"

"id": "np1-family-implementation-evaluation",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation",
"body": "## What it is\nImplementation is carrying out the family care plan, mainly through home visits and clinic contact, and evaluation is judging whether the goals were met. Both steps involve the family as an active partner.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Main implementation tools: home visits, health education, demonstration and return demonstration, referrals, and coordination with other workers and agencies. Documentation also takes place during implementation.\n- The home visit is a professional family-nurse contact that lets the nurse assess the home and family situation and give care. Principles: it must have a purpose or objective; planning should use all available information, involve the individual and family, and give priority to essential needs.\n- Purposes of a home visit: give nursing care, assess living conditions, teach prevention and control of disease, build a close relationship between health agencies and the public, and use the referral system.\n- Factors that decide how often to visit: the needs of the family (the most important), acceptance by the family (second), agency policy, other agencies involved, past services given, and the family's ability to recognize its own needs.\n- Steps of the visit: greet and introduce yourself; state the purpose; observe the patient and determine health needs; place the bag in a convenient place and perform bag technique; give the needed nursing care and health teaching; record important data, observations and care given; make an appointment for the return visit.\n- The bag technique helps prevent cross-infection during home visits: place the bag on a clean surface, wash hands before and after care, and avoid contaminating the inside of the bag. The bag should hold the supplies and equipment for emergency needs, be cleaned often and be ready at all times; arrangement should suit the user. Check the exact steps in your textbook.\n- Evaluation compares results with objectives. Ask: was the objective met, partially met, or not met? The handout names three classic frameworks for evaluating: structure elements (resources and setting), process elements (what was done and how) and outcome elements (the results).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Review records and confirm the objectives of the visit before leaving the office.\n2. Greet the family and explain the purpose; consent and trust come first.\n3. Wash hands, use the bag technique, and provide care and teaching in order of the priority problem.\n4. Document the visit in the family folder immediately, because follow-up depends on it.\n5. Plan the next visit based on the evaluation; if the goal is unmet, reassess and revise.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to priority action: if an objective is not met, find the cause first (was the plan wrong, the family unable, the resources missing?) before repeating the same intervention.\n- Order: prepare before the visit (review and objectives), assess on arrival (observe, find needs), then intervene, document and schedule. Assess before you act; plan before you go.\n- Frequency of visits follows need first, then acceptance, so a family with a newborn danger sign gets visited sooner than a stable family regardless of the schedule.\n- For infection control, clean-to-dirty and hands first: the bag is protected from the home environment and the nurse protects both the family and herself.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nUse teach-back and return demonstration to confirm understanding. Teach the family to do the tasks themselves so care continues between visits.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems ask what to do first before a home visit (review the family record and set objectives), what is wrong with a nurse's technique, which factor most influences visit frequency, or whether an objective was met. A trap is choosing a nursing action before getting the family's agreement or before reviewing data. Another trap: ranking agency policy above family need.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Planning and Prioritization]], [[Family Nursing Process: Assessment]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Home Visit and Bag Technique]], [[Community Health Nursing Process: Assessment to Evaluation]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe step-by-step bag technique and the standard forms used in the family folder vary across schools and local health offices; verify local policy. The ranking of needs and acceptance as first and second factors for visit frequency comes from the handout; confirm it.\n",

"id": "np1-health-promotion",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise",
"body": "## What it is\nHealth promotion helps people increase control over their health and improve it. It is aimed at healthy people and at those at risk, and it works through education, supportive environments and healthy public policy. It differs from disease prevention mainly in motive: promotion builds wellness; prevention avoids a specific disease.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The Ottawa Charter (1986) is the best-known international statement, listing action areas such as building healthy public policy, creating supportive environments, strengthening community action, developing personal skills, and reorienting health services. Verify wording in your reviewer.\n- Promotion belongs to the first level of prevention (primary). The handout's examples of primary prevention are immunization and promotion of a healthy lifestyle through proper diet and exercise.\n- Pender's health promotion model is the usual nursing theory for well clients: it looks at personal factors, perceived benefits and barriers, and self-efficacy to explain why people adopt healthy behavior. The health belief model (perceived susceptibility, severity, benefits and barriers) is another common frame. Verify which your school uses.\n- Diet: a balanced diet uses all food groups in proper proportion. Filipino food guides (such as a plate model used by the DOH or national nutrition authorities) show portions of go, grow and glow foods; verify the current name and shape. Advise less salt, sugar and saturated fat, more vegetables and fruit, and enough water. Breastfeeding is the model for infant feeding.\n- Exercise: regular moderate activity on most days lowers the risk of heart disease, diabetes, obesity and depression. A widely used adult guide is about 150 minutes of moderate activity per week, but verify with your textbook. Start gradually, warm up, keep hydrated, and stop with chest pain, severe breathlessness or dizziness.\n- Common lifestyle risks to address: tobacco, alcohol, unhealthy diet, physical inactivity, unsafe sex and stress; these drive the lifestyle-related diseases that the national program targets.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess lifestyle, readiness to change and barriers.\n2. Agree on a short, specific plan; small goals succeed better than big ones.\n3. Teach and demonstrate; link advice to the person's own foods, work and budget.\n4. Follow up and reinforce.\n5. Refer clients with chronic illness for clearance before they begin vigorous exercise.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a client who knows the risks but does not change has a barrier or low confidence, so the answer is a small, doable step plus support, not repeating the lecture.\n- Order: assess readiness before teaching; safety (medical clearance, danger signs) before intensity; promote health in the well before treating the sick, because promotion reaches the most people early.\n- Pick the choice that is specific, realistic and uses the client's own resources over generic advice.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nUse group sessions, posters, school programs and local examples. Involve the family in cooking and activity choices.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems ask which activity is promotion rather than prevention, or the best teaching for a client with a specific habit. Pick answers that respect readiness and give practical steps. A trap: calling screening (for example blood sugar testing) health promotion; it is secondary prevention.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Nutrition]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Current national food-guide model and activity targets: verify local policy. Pender and health belief model details differ between books.\n",

"id": "np1-stop-smoking",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation",
"body": "## What it is\nTobacco use is a leading preventable cause of heart disease, lung disease and cancer. Cessation counselling is a core health-promotion skill for the nurse in the community, and a lifestyle-change example of primary prevention for non-smokers and secondary or tertiary prevention for those already harmed.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Brief counselling model, the 5 As: Ask about tobacco use, Advise to quit clearly, Assess willingness to quit, Assist with a plan and support, Arrange follow-up.\n- For clients not ready, the 5 Rs boost motivation: Relevance, Risks, Rewards, Roadblocks, Repetition.\n- Stages of change: precontemplation (not thinking of quitting), contemplation (thinking, ambivalent), preparation (planning soon), action (quit recently), maintenance (staying quit). Relapse may occur and is part of the process. Match the intervention to the stage.\n- Withdrawal symptoms are real: irritability, craving, anxiety, poor concentration, increased appetite. They are strongest in the first days and fade.\n- Aids include nicotine replacement and prescription medicines (such as bupropion or varenicline); these are decided by a clinician. Quit lines and support groups are community resources.\n- Cigarette smoke contains nicotine (addictive), tar and carbon monoxide. Smoking raises the risk of COPD, lung and other cancers, coronary disease and stroke; it harms the fetus in pregnancy.\n- Secondhand smoke harms children, pregnant women and the elderly; smoke-free homes protect families.\n- Philippine tools usually cited: the Tobacco Regulation Act (RA 9211) and a national smoke-free policy by executive order; verify numbers and current rules.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Ask and document tobacco use at every contact, because identification leads to action.\n2. Assess readiness; counselling that ignores readiness fails.\n3. If ready: set a quit date, remove tobacco items, identify triggers, plan substitutes and arrange follow-up.\n4. If not ready: motivate with the 5 Rs, without pressure.\n5. Praise any success; treat relapse as learning and encourage another try.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: the client's own statement reveals the stage. \"I do not think smoking is a problem\" is precontemplation, so give personalized information; \"I will quit next week\" is preparation, so help set a date and plan.\n- Order: assess readiness before advising steps, advise before medicines, and follow up after. The reason is that behavior change is driven by the client's readiness, not by the nurse's urgency.\n- In a safety case (chest pain, breathlessness in a smoker) treat the acute problem first, then counsel.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nTeach triggers, delaying tactics (drink water, walk, breathe deeply) and avoiding smoking cues. Involve family and workplace support. Expect weight gain and cravings and plan for them.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems describe a smoker with statements showing a stage. Choose the intervention for that stage (information for contemplation, a plan for preparation, relapse prevention for maintenance). A trap is lecturing someone in precontemplation about quit steps. Another trap: calling a relapse a failure rather than part of the cycle.\n\n## Memory aid\nStandard: Ask, Advise, Assess, Assist, Arrange.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Philippine anti-smoking laws and their provisions, smoke-free areas and graphic warning rules: verify local policy; I am not certain of the current details.\n- Which medicines are available in public programs: verify.\n",

"title": "Magna Carta of Public Health Workers",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Magna Carta of Public Health Workers is a Philippine law that sets out the rights, benefits and protection of workers in government health service, including those in rural and remote areas. It aims to attract and keep health workers in public service and recognize their role in delivering health care.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Public health workers covered are generally those engaged in health or health-related work in the government, including nurses, midwives, doctors and other staff in health facilities and agencies. Check the exact definition.\n- Benefits commonly associated with the law include additional pay or allowances for hardship (such as assignment in remote areas), hazard pay for risk of exposure, subsistence and laundry allowances, longevity pay, protection of security of tenure, and rights to training and career development. Verify exact amounts, conditions and eligibility in current rules.\n- The law also describes rights such as freedom to organize, and standards on working hours and leave. Verify details.\n- It is commonly cited as Republic Act 7305, enacted in the early 1990s; verify the number and date, and check later amendments.\n- Link to other topics: the rules implementing the law define which barangays count as far-flung, depressed or hard to reach. The Botika ng Barangay program uses that definition to choose its sites, and the public health nurse starts at Salary Grade 15 in the handout.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Know your rights and duties as a government health worker, because exam items test application, such as which worker qualifies for hazard pay.\n2. Keep records and report hazards, to protect yourself and others.\n3. Use the law as a basis for advocacy within your agency.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: match the worker's situation to the benefit's purpose. Risk of exposure points to hazard pay; remote assignment points to hardship allowance; long service points to longevity pay; meals and uniform-related costs point to subsistence and laundry allowances.\n- Order: identify the condition in the stem first, then the benefit; do not apply every benefit to every worker. The reason is that each benefit pays for a specific burden.\n- When the question is about protecting the worker (tenure, organization), choose the rights provision rather than a pay provision.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems ask which benefit applies in a scenario (a nurse assigned to a remote barangay, a worker exposed to infectious disease) or what the law is for. Match the benefit to the situation; do not assume every benefit applies to every worker. A trap: choosing the Magna Carta for private-hospital nurses; it concerns government health workers.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions]], [[Community Health Nursing Basics]], [[Botika ng Barangay]], [[Public Health Nurse Roles and Qualifications]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nI am not certain of the law's current numbers, allowance amounts or later updates, so treat every number and condition here as verify local policy. "id": "np1-phc-basics",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Primary Health Care Basics",
"body": "## What it is\nPrimary health care (PHC) is essential health care that is made universally accessible to individuals and families in the community, by means acceptable to them, through their full participation and at a cost the community and country can afford. It is both a level of care (the first contact) and a philosophy and strategy for achieving health for all.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The Alma-Ata Declaration (1978), from the WHO and UNICEF conference, endorsed PHC as the key to health for all. The Philippines adopted PHC through Letter of Instruction (LOI) 949, signed by President Marcos on October 19, 1979, under the theme \"Health in the Hands of the People by 2020\" (as the handout states; verify).\n- The handout characterizes PHC as partnership and empowerment of the people, the core strategy for delivering essential services that are community-based, accessible, acceptable and sustainable at a cost the community and government can afford.\n- PHC is the first level of contact between individuals and the health system and the first element of a continuing health care process.\n- PHC addresses the main health problems of the community with promotive, preventive, curative and rehabilitative services.\n- Health is seen as part of overall social and economic development, so PHC works with other sectors such as education, agriculture and housing.\n- Primary health care workers, by level: village or barangay health workers (trained community health workers, health auxiliary volunteers, traditional birth attendants and healers), and intermediate-level workers (general medical practitioners or their assistants, for example public health nurses, rural sanitary inspectors, midwives and the rural health physician).\n- The eight elements of PHC used in the Philippines and the four pillars have their own notes.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Make care available at the community level first, so people use the nearest accessible service.\n2. Promote community participation in planning and running services.\n3. Use appropriate technology that the community understands and can maintain.\n4. Link with other sectors, because many health determinants lie outside the health sector.\n5. Refer upward when needed.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a barangay with poor access, low immunization and unsafe water needs services brought near and people involved, not a new hospital. PHC is chosen because it reaches the most people at an affordable cost.\n- Order of preference: community-based over hospital-based, prevention and promotion over cure, participation over delivering services to passive people, simple appropriate technology over high technology.\n- Distinguish PHC (strategy and philosophy) from primary level of care (the first-contact facility such as RHU or barangay health station); questions can target either.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nTeach self-care and basic prevention at home, and encourage families to join health committees and activities.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems ask what PHC emphasizes (prevention and community participation), the best example of PHC, or which declaration launched it. Trap: choosing a hospital-based, high-technology option. Another trap: confusing Alma-Ata (international, 1978) with LOI 949 (Philippine, 1979).\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: A-A-A-A for Accessible, Acceptable, Affordable, Appropriate.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Pillars and Characteristics]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Community Health Nursing Basics]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWording of the definition and its Philippine legal basis: verify local policy and your reviewer. The theme year \"by 2020\" and the exact date of LOI 949 come from the handout; verify.\n",

"area": "NP1",
"title": "Primary Health Care Pillars and Characteristics",
"body": "## What it is\nThe pillars (cornerstones or strategies) of PHC are the foundations that make it work. The characteristics describe what good PHC services look like.\n\n## Must-know facts\nThe four cornerstones in the handout (the usual Philippine list):\n1. Active community participation: people take part in decisions and actions.\n2. Intra- and inter-sectoral linkages: cooperation within the health sector and between health and other sectors.\n3. Use of appropriate technology: simple, affordable, culturally acceptable and sustainable.\n4. Support mechanisms made available: financial, manpower, logistical and other resources to sustain the program.\nSome books present these as three pillars or use slightly different names. Verify against your reviewer.\n\nCharacteristics of PHC as commonly listed: accessible (geographically, financially, culturally), acceptable, affordable, available, appropriate, equitable, comprehensive, community-based, participatory and sustainable. The handout's wording is essential health services that are community-based, accessible, acceptable and sustainable at a cost the community and government can afford. Your textbook may name a subset.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Involve the community from the start; participation comes first because ownership drives the other pillars.\n2. Build partnerships with schools, local government, churches and non-government groups.\n3. Choose simple, low-cost methods that people can use after you leave, such as home preparation of oral rehydration solution.\n4. Secure supplies and training so services last.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if a program fails after outside funding ends, support mechanisms and ownership were weak; strengthen participation first, then secure local resources.\n- Order: people first (participation), partners second (linkages), tools third (technology), resources fourth (support). The reason is that without ownership, partners and tools are not used.\n- Match scenario words to pillar: villagers decide or volunteer is participation; DepEd, DPWH, agriculture or NGOs mean intersectoral; simple, locally made, culturally fitting is appropriate technology; budget, trained staff, supplies, supervision is support mechanism.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Intersectoral collaboration: for example, working with agriculture on nutrition or with public works on water supply. Intrasectoral means among units within the health sector.\n- Appropriate technology: fits local culture and resources.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nItems give a scenario and ask which pillar is shown (for example, a health center teaming up with the school and the barangay council is intersectoral linkage). Trap: confusing participation (people decide) with support mechanisms (resources provided). Another trap: calling an expensive machine appropriate technology.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: P-I-T-S for Participation, Intersectoral, Technology, Support.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Community Organizing (COPAR) Basic Principles]], [[Local Health System and Inter-Local Health Zones]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWhether your school teaches three or four pillars and the exact list of characteristics: verify.\n",

"title": "Elements of Primary Health Care",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Philippine approach to PHC is built on eight service elements that together cover the main community health needs. They are the content of PHC delivery at the barangay and RHU.\n\n## Must-know facts\nThe eight elements (a common memory aid is the word ELEMENTS):\n- Education for health\n- Locally endemic and communicable disease control and treatment\n- Expanded program on immunization\n- Maternal and child health and family planning\n- Essential drugs\n- Nutrition\n- Treatment (medical and emergency care, non-communicable diseases and mental health)\n- Sanitation of the environment\nSome lists reorder or split them (for example separating communicable and non-communicable disease treatment). Verify.\n\nEducation sits first because every other element depends on people understanding and accepting the service.\n\nQuick matching of activities to elements: teaching families about handwashing is education; spraying and case finding for dengue or malaria is locally endemic disease control; BCG, measles and pentavalent doses are EPI; prenatal visits, tetanus toxoid and birth spacing are maternal and child health and family planning; generic drugs, herbal plants and Botika ng Barangay are essential drugs; vitamin A, iron and breastfeeding are nutrition; first aid, hypertension care and mental health service are treatment; toilets, safe water and waste disposal are sanitation.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Use health education in every contact, because it supports all the other elements.\n2. Prioritize by community need using local data (for example, high diarrhea rates mean emphasize sanitation and oral rehydration).\n3. Integrate services: one visit can cover immunization, nutrition and teaching.\n4. Monitor coverage and report.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a community pattern points to the element to strengthen. Many children with worms and diarrhea means sanitation and education; many underweight children means nutrition and complementary feeding teaching; outbreaks of measles means EPI coverage and defaulter tracing.\n- Order: education first, because behavior change is the base of all services, then the element the data points to.\n- When an activity fits two elements, choose the one the question's purpose names (Vitamin A given to prevent blindness is nutrition; given with measles vaccine as a routine contact is still a nutrition service).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nItems name an activity (for example, giving vitamin A, building toilets, distributing generic drugs) and ask which element it belongs to. Match each activity to the single best element, and watch for overlaps such as nutrition versus maternal and child health. A trap: placing emergency care under essential drugs.\n\n## Memory aid\nThe word ELEMENTS: Education, Locally endemic disease control, EPI, Maternal and child health, Essential drugs, Nutrition, Treatment, Sanitation. This word-to-element mapping is a common device that I reconstructed here; compare it with your own reviewer.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Epidemiology and Endemic Diseases]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Nutrition]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Essential Drugs]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nExact element names, order and wording of the treatment element (for example, whether emergency services are included): verify local policy and your reviewer.\n",

"id": "np1-sentrong-sigla",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Sentrong Sigla Program",
"body": "## What it is\nSentrong Sigla is a Philippine DOH quality assurance and certification program for health facilities and services. Its purpose is to encourage health centers to meet set standards so that the public receives safe, quality service. Facilities that meet the standards are recognized, and the movement encourages continuous improvement. The handout states the goal as quality health care, services and facilities.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- It focuses on quality of service at the primary care level, such as RHUs and health centers.\n- Three levels of recognition in the handout: Basic Sentrong Sigla certification (minimum input, process and output standards for integrated public health services for the core programs, the facility system, regulatory functions and basic curative services); Specialty Awards (second-level quality standards for selected core public health programs); and Awards for Excellence (the highest level, requiring that Level 2 standards be maintained for the core programs, and a Level 2 facility system, for at least three consecutive years).\n- Certification is valid for two years. Facilities that keep their current level without advancing receive stickers confirming renewal of the seal, with no extra incentives. Facilities that slide back keep the seal but do not get a sticker.\n- Structure of the Level I standards: primary function is basic public health services; facility and system standards (enough staff, logistics and organized procedures); integrated public health function standards (promote programs and control problems through direct client care); basic curative function standards (primary-level outpatient and emergency care); regulatory function standards (an environment that prevents and reduces community risks and hazards).\n- Level II: local health system development, plus integrated public health functions covering five core programs: integrated women's health, child care, prevention and control of infectious disease, integrated prevention and control of lifestyle-related diseases, and environmental health. The handout also says four core programs at Level I and for the specialty awards; the counts are inconsistent, so verify.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Know the standards your health center is measured against so that care is consistent.\n2. Prepare records, equipment and the facility environment for assessment.\n3. Continue to improve after recognition; certification is not the end.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a facility that fails an assessment has a gap in staff, supplies, procedures or program results; fix the gap named by the standard, not the sticker.\n- Order of levels: basic, then specialty awards, then excellence. Each level builds on the one before, so a facility cannot skip to excellence without sustained results.\n- It is quality assurance, not funding: choose it for questions about recognizing and improving facility standards.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nItems ask what the program is for (quality assurance and recognition of health facilities), which agency runs it (the DOH), or what happens at renewal. A trap: confusing it with a financing or insurance program (PhilHealth). Another trap: thinking a facility that slides back loses its seal immediately; per the handout it only loses the sticker.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions]], [[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Health Sector Reform Agenda and FOURmula One]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe exact standards, levels of recognition, the program's history and its present status or renamed forms: verify local policy. I am not certain which of these are current. The core-program counts (four versus five) in the handout are inconsistent.\n",

"id": "np1-competency-based-teaching",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Competency-Based Teaching",
"body": "## What it is\nEducation for health is the first PHC element. Competency-based teaching plans teaching around what the learner must be able to do at the end, in terms of knowledge, attitude and skill, and then checks whether they can really do it. It is learner-centered and outcome-focused.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Learning domains: cognitive (knowing), affective (attitude and values), psychomotor (skills). A competency usually combines all three.\n- Steps: assess learning needs and readiness, state measurable outcomes, choose methods that match the domain, teach, then evaluate using observation or return demonstration.\n- Match method to domain: explanation, lecture and discussion for knowledge; role play, group discussion and testimonials for attitudes; demonstration and return demonstration for skills.\n- Adults learn best when content is relevant, practical, builds on experience and answers their own needs.\n- Evaluation looks at behavior and performance, not only test scores. Levels: knowledge gained, attitude changed, skill performed, then behavior sustained at home.\n- Teaching aids should be simple, local, visible and tested before use.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the learner first (what they know, can do and believe; literacy and language), because teaching without assessment wastes time.\n2. Set clear, measurable, learner-centered objectives with the learner.\n3. Choose method and materials to match the domain and audience.\n4. Teach in small steps and check understanding.\n5. Evaluate with return demonstration or teach-back; reteach if the competency is not shown.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a mother who can state ORS steps but mixes it wrongly has the knowledge but not the skill, so the next step is demonstration and return demonstration, not repeating the lecture.\n- Order: assess readiness, then objective, then method, then teach, then evaluate. The reason is that the objective decides what counts as success and the method follows the domain.\n- Choose active over passive and specific over general; the best evaluation is the one that observes the actual behavior.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nUse local language, pictures, demonstrations with household items and short sessions. Teach caregivers the exact skill they will need, such as preparing oral rehydration solution.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nItems give an outcome (for example, a mother must be able to prepare ORS) and ask which teaching method or evaluation fits. The answer is usually demonstration followed by return demonstration. Trap: choosing a lecture or a pamphlet for a skill. Another trap: picking a written test to evaluate a psychomotor competency.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Levels of Prevention]], [[Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Health Education and Competency-Based Teaching]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTerms such as competency-based versus outcome-based, and how your school defines the domains: verify. This note has no matching handout section; the content is general teaching-learning principles.\n",
