"id": "np1-levels-of-prevention",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Levels of Prevention",
"body": "## What it is\nPrevention is organized in three levels according to the stage of disease at which action is taken. Identifying the correct level of a given nursing action is a very common exam skill.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Primary prevention: before disease occurs. Goal: health promotion and specific protection. Examples: health teaching, immunization, promotion of a healthy lifestyle (proper diet and exercise), nutrition, safe water and sanitation, seat belts, family planning, prenatal care, handwashing, avoiding tobacco.\n- Secondary prevention: early diagnosis and prompt treatment to stop progress and limit complications. The handout's examples: breast self-examination, diagnostic tests such as the AFB sputum test, and recognition of cancer warning signs (CAUTION US). Others: Pap smear, mammography, blood pressure and blood sugar screening, newborn screening, case finding, contact tracing and early treatment.\n- Tertiary prevention: after a disease or disability exists. Goal: limit disability, prevent further disability and restore the client's optimum level of functioning (rehabilitation). The handout's examples: mental health care, crutch walking, physical therapy. Others: stroke rehabilitation, colostomy teaching, cardiac rehabilitation, vocational training.\n- Immunization is primary prevention. Health promotion is usually counted within primary prevention. Some books describe an extra level called primordial prevention; verify.\n- CAUTION US stands for change in bowel or bladder habits, a sore that does not heal, unusual bleeding or discharge, thickening or lump, indigestion or difficulty swallowing, obvious change in a wart or mole, nagging cough or hoarseness, unexplained anemia, and sudden unexplained weight loss.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify where the client is in the natural history of disease. Healthy or at risk: primary. Disease present but early: secondary. Established disease with damage: tertiary.\n2. Choose the intervention for that level.\n3. Remember that one disease can involve all three levels at different times (for example diabetes: diet and exercise to prevent it, screening to detect it, foot care and eye exams to prevent complications).\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: the question's wording tells the stage. \"At risk\" or \"healthy\" means primary; \"detected\" or \"screened\" means secondary; \"already has\" or \"disability\" means tertiary.\n- Order of effort: primary is cheapest and reaches the most people, so a community program prefers it when the stem asks what is best for a healthy population; for a person with early disease the priority is secondary (treat now), and for damage the priority is tertiary (limit disability).\n- Judge by timing relative to the disease, not by who does it or where; a nurse teaching in a clinic can be doing any level.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Health promotion is general; specific protection acts against a particular disease, such as a vaccine.\n- Early diagnosis and prompt treatment are the heart of secondary prevention.\n- Disability limitation and rehabilitation belong to tertiary prevention.\n- Pre-pathogenesis (susceptibility) matches primary; pre-symptomatic and early lesions match secondary; advanced disease and disability match tertiary.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give an action and ask for the level. Ask yourself: has the disease already occurred? If no, primary. If it is present and being found early, secondary. If damage is already present, tertiary. Trap: calling screening primary prevention. Another trap: calling breast self-examination primary because it is done at home; it detects disease, so it is secondary.\n\n## Memory aid\nInvented by me: Prevent it, Catch it, Limit it.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise]], [[Stop Smoking and Tobacco Cessation]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Three Levels of Prevention]], [[Epidemiology: Multiple Causation, Natural History and Four Approaches]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWhether your school treats health promotion as a separate level or counts early diagnosis within secondary prevention: verify. The handout lists mental health under tertiary prevention without explanation; classify by the situation in the stem.\n",

"prevention",

"secondary",
"tertiary"

"Identify the Prevention",
"Three Levels of Prevention"

"id": "np1-elements-of-phc",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Eight Elements of Primary Health Care: Overview",
"body": "## What it is\nThe \"elements\" are the eight basic services that primary health care (PHC) delivers at community level. Nursing review decks compress them into the word ELEMENTS. Each letter is a service area, and each has its own note in this set. PHC in the Philippines was adopted through Letter of Instruction 949 (1979) with the theme \"Health in the Hands of the People by 2020\": essential services that are community-based, accessible, acceptable and affordable, built on partnership and empowerment of the people.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- E: Education for health (health teaching, prevention)\n- L: Locally endemic and communicable disease control and treatment (diseases common in the area, plus the data used to track them)\n- E: Expanded Program on Immunization\n- M: Maternal and child health, including family planning\n- E: Essential drugs (generic and herbal medicines)\n- N: Nutrition\n- T: Treatment of common diseases and injuries: medical and emergency care, noncommunicable diseases and mental health\n- S: Sanitation of the environment (safe water, toilets, waste, food safety)\n- The Mental Health Program is placed under \"T\" in the handout version; many reviewers also teach it as a ninth item. See the last note in this set.\n- The four cornerstones (pillars) that make PHC work: active community participation, intra- and inter-sectoral linkages, use of appropriate technology, and support mechanisms made available. Participation is the pillar the exam likes most.\n- Who delivers PHC: village-level workers (barangay health workers, trained community volunteers, traditional birth attendants or healers) and intermediate-level workers (public health nurse, midwife, rural sanitary inspector, rural health physician).\n- Where it is delivered: the primary level of care (barangay health station, rural health unit, health center), devolved to cities and municipalities under the Local Government Code (RA 7160). The two-way referral system links it to secondary and tertiary hospitals.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the community first: which element is the biggest gap (for example low immunization coverage or unsafe water)? Assessing before acting is the nursing-process rule.\n2. Prioritize problems that threaten life or spread quickly (outbreaks, unsafe water, a measles cluster) before slower problems such as dental caries. Unstable and fast-spreading come before stable and slow.\n3. Deliver the service through the barangay health station or rural health unit, with the midwife, barangay health workers and community.\n4. Teach and organize the community to sustain the service themselves. PHC stresses community participation, not nurse-only delivery.\n5. Refer what is beyond the health center, and refer back for follow-up (two-way referral).\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Primary level of care: first contact, closest to home, lowest cost.\n- Referral: the nurse sends clients to higher levels when the service is beyond the health center.\n- Appropriate technology: simple, affordable, culturally acceptable tools the community can maintain (the vaccine carrier, ORS packets, a pour-flush toilet).\n- Typical activity-to-element sorting: vaccine cold box = EPI; ORS demonstration = Treatment (or Education, if the point is the teaching); weighing of preschoolers = Nutrition; iron-folic acid in pregnancy = Maternal and child health; latrine construction = Sanitation; herbal garden = Essential drugs; sputum collection and DOTS = Locally endemic disease control.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which element does this activity belong to?\" Decide by the main purpose of the activity.\n- A trap: teaching is part of every element, but \"Education\" is the element only when the activity itself is the health teaching.\n- Another trap: \"Which is NOT an element?\" Distractors are usually hospital-level services (surgery, intensive care) or curative-only services.\n- \"Which pillar is shown?\" Barangay residents choosing and running a project = community participation; health center working with the school and the DepEd = intersectoral linkage.\n\n## Memory aid\nELEMENTS (E-L-E-M-E-N-T-S) is the common reviewer mnemonic described above.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Basics|Primary Health Care]], [[Primary Health Care Pillars and Characteristics]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]], [[Health Education and Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Essential Drugs]], [[Nutrition]], [[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]], [[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]], [[Community Mental Health Program]], [[Sentrong Sigla Program]], [[Botika ng Barangay]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some books word the letters slightly differently (for example \"Safe water and sanitation\"). Verify the wording your school uses.\n- Whether your school treats mental health as part of \"T\" or as its own element, and whether oral health is added to the list (verify local policy).\n- The year in the PHC theme (\"by 2020\") appears in older materials; newer documents use different targets.",

"id": "np1-health-education",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Health Education and Competency-Based Teaching",
"body": "## What it is\nHealth education is the first element of PHC. It helps people gain knowledge, change attitudes and practice healthy behavior. Competency-based teaching means teaching until the learner can actually perform the skill or behavior, not only recite facts. In the clinic-visit standard of the rural health unit, one-on-one counseling is the last step of every visit, and in the home visit the nurse gives health teaching after the care itself.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Three learning domains: cognitive (knowing), affective (feeling or valuing), psychomotor (doing).\n- Competency-based teaching defines the exact behavior the learner must show at the end (for example, mixes ORS correctly), then teaches toward it and checks it by return demonstration.\n- Teach-back is the strongest simple check of understanding: the learner explains it in their own words.\n- Good teaching starts from the learner's readiness, culture, language and existing practices.\n- Match the method to the domain: lecture for cognitive, demonstration for psychomotor, discussion, role play or testimonials for affective.\n- Verbs in an objective point to the domain. Cognitive: list, explain, identify. Affective: accept, value, appreciate, express willingness. Psychomotor: demonstrate, prepare, perform, apply.\n- Learning principles that appear in exams: the learner must be ready and motivated; repetition and reinforcement strengthen retention; active participation beats passive listening; teach simple to complex and known to unknown; immediate feedback helps.\n- Adults learn best when the topic solves a problem they already feel; children learn through play, pictures and doing.\n- Common teaching channels in the community: one-on-one counseling, group discussion, demonstration, home visit, posters and flyers, radio and mass media, and trained barangay health workers. Mass media reaches many people but changes behavior less than demonstration and personal contact.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess learning needs, literacy, beliefs and readiness first. Teaching someone who is not ready, or in a language they do not use, wastes effort. If the client is in pain, anxious or in an emergency, treat that first and teach later (Maslow: physical and safety needs before learning).\n2. Set measurable objectives (what the learner will be able to do), one per domain needed.\n3. Choose the method and aids that fit the objective and the group.\n4. Teach in simple steps, from simple to complex and known to unknown.\n5. Evaluate by observation, return demonstration or teach-back, then reinforce and document.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Return demonstration: learner performs the skill while the nurse watches.\n- Community participation: people help plan and run teaching activities.\n- Barangay health workers can be trained to multiply the nurse's teaching.\n- Health teaching moments built into services: at prenatal and postpartum visits (danger signs, breastfeeding), at immunization sessions (what to expect, return date), at the sputum or DOTS visit (finish treatment), at the home visit.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nFocus on a few key messages with demonstrations (handwashing, ORS preparation, breastfeeding position) in the local language, involving the mother and other decision makers in the family. Use local examples, check the message was understood, and agree on one action the family will do before the next visit.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which shows teaching was effective?\" The answer is usually a behavior shown (return demonstration) rather than attendance or \"the client said yes\".\n- Match the objective's verb to the domain. A verb like \"demonstrate\" points to psychomotor; \"state the reasons\" is cognitive; \"express willingness\" is affective.\n- \"What is the first step before teaching?\" Assess readiness and learning needs.\n- Trap: a lecture cannot show that a psychomotor objective was met.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Three Levels of Prevention]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Primary Health Care Basics|Primary Health Care]], [[Clinic Visit Standard Procedures]], [[Home Visit and Bag Technique]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact names of teaching models or frameworks your school uses (verify local policy).\n- The domain verb lists are a common teaching convention; your school's taxonomy may differ.\n- The statement about mass media being weaker than demonstration is a general teaching principle, not taken from the handout.",

"id": "np1-three-levels-of-prevention",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Three Levels of Prevention",
"body": "## What it is\nPrevention is organized by when it acts in the natural history of disease. Exam items give an activity and ask which level it belongs to.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Primary prevention: before disease occurs. Health promotion (nutrition teaching, exercise, healthy lifestyle) and specific protection (immunization, handwashing, safe water, helmet use, condoms).\n- Secondary prevention: early detection and prompt treatment to stop progression (screening such as blood pressure checks, Pap smear, breast self-examination, diagnostic tests such as the sputum AFB test, case finding, recognizing early cancer warning signs, prompt treatment of a diagnosed case).\n- Tertiary prevention: after disease or disability is established. Limit disability and rehabilitate, restoring the client's best level of functioning (physical therapy after stroke, crutch walking, mental health rehabilitation, teaching a client with diabetes to prevent foot complications).\n- The same disease can have all three levels. Pick the level from the client's current stage, not from the disease name. Tuberculosis example: BCG and ventilation = primary; sputum exam of a coughing contact = secondary; supervised treatment to prevent complications and relapse, plus lung rehabilitation = tertiary.\n- Link to the natural history of disease: pre-pathogenesis (susceptible, no disease yet) = primary; early pathogenesis (no symptoms yet, or early lesions) = secondary; advanced disease, disability and recovery = tertiary.\n- Some books split the three levels into five: health promotion and specific protection (primary), early diagnosis and prompt treatment (secondary, which also includes disability limitation in some versions), and rehabilitation (tertiary).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify where the client is: healthy and at risk, ill but undiagnosed, or diagnosed with complications.\n2. Choose the activity that matches that stage.\n3. Prefer higher up the chain (primary first) when the goal is community impact, because it is cheapest and prevents suffering.\n4. Apply all three levels in one program: promote and protect everyone, screen those at risk, and rehabilitate those affected.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Health promotion vs specific protection: both primary; promotion is general (nutrition education), protection is aimed at one disease (vaccine).\n- Screening is secondary even when the person has no symptoms.\n- Clue words: \"to avoid getting\" = primary; \"to detect early\" or \"prompt treatment\" = secondary; \"to prevent further disability\", \"restore function\" or \"relapse\" = tertiary.\n- Quick sorting examples: breast self-examination = secondary; Pap smear = secondary; clean cord care = primary; physical therapy = tertiary; antenatal iron-folic acid = primary; treating a diagnosed case of dengue promptly = secondary.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Identify the prevention\": a short scenario of an activity; ask yourself, \"Is the person already diagnosed? Is the aim to find disease early or to prevent it or to limit damage?\"\n- Trap: read the stem for what stage is described; the same vaccine-like or teaching activity can sit at different levels depending on the client's status.\n- Trap: rehabilitation is tertiary, even though it feels like \"treatment\".\n- Trap: teaching a diabetic about foot care is tertiary, but teaching a healthy student about balanced diet is primary.\n\n## Memory aid\nPrimary = Prevent, Secondary = Seek early, Tertiary = Take care of damage. This is my own invented aid.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Health Education and Competency-Based Teaching]], [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Community Mental Health Program]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Some textbooks list health promotion and specific protection under primary; others label them separately. Verify how your school groups them.\n- Whether \"prompt treatment\" and \"disability limitation\" are secondary or tertiary differs between books (verify your school's version).",

"id": "np1-epidemiology-fhsis",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Epidemiology and FHSIS",
"body": "## What it is\nEpidemiology studies how often, where and why disease occurs in populations (occurrence, distribution and determinants), then uses that knowledge to control it. The Field Health Services Information System (FHSIS) is the Philippine health-facility reporting system that gathers service and disease data from health centers upward to municipal, provincial and national levels.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Multiple causation: a disease arises from several factors acting together, pictured as the wheel, the web or the ecologic triad. The triad is agent, host, environment (biological, social and physical). Control targets whichever link is easiest to break.\n- Natural history of disease: pre-pathogenesis (susceptibility), then pathogenesis in three sub-stages (pre-symptomatic, discernible lesions, advanced disease), ending in recovery, disability or death. Each stage matches a level of prevention.\n- Four approaches: descriptive (who, where, when: person, place, time), analytic (tests why, through hypotheses), experimental or intervention (does a new control method work?), and evaluation (do services and programs work?).\n- Surveillance is continuous collection, analysis and use of data for action.\n- Patterns of occurrence: endemic (usual constant presence; handout examples are schistosomiasis in Leyte and Samar, filariasis in Sorsogon, malaria in Palawan), epidemic or outbreak (unusually large number of cases in a short time), pandemic (epidemic in several countries; HIV/AIDS and SARS), sporadic (few, scattered, unrelated cases; rabies is the textbook example).\n- FHSIS objectives: summary data on service delivery, data for monitoring and evaluation, a standard facility-level database, accurate and useful data, and the least recording and reporting burden at the service level.\n- FHSIS components, in the order the exam uses them: the Family Treatment Record (the fundamental building block), the Target Client List (the second building block), reporting forms (the only routine route by which data move from one facility to another; monthly or quarterly) and output reports (designed to be useful for monitoring and management).\n- Data must be accurate, complete and timely to be useful.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Record every client contact accurately at the time of service; unrecorded care disappears from planning data.\n2. Compile the monthly report on time and check it before sending.\n3. Analyze trends locally (are cases rising? is coverage dropping?) and act.\n4. Report notifiable diseases promptly through the surveillance system, because delays allow spread. In an outbreak, control measures start while the investigation continues; do not wait for the full report.\n5. Feed results back to the community and barangay officials.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Outbreak investigation steps (general): verify the diagnosis, confirm the outbreak, define cases, find cases, describe by person-place-time, form a hypothesis, test it, control, report.\n- Notifiable disease: law requires reporting (RA 11332 is the usual reference).\n- Family Treatment Record: one record per family member, kept at the health station; Target Client List: a program-based list that tracks each eligible client (for example all pregnant women or all infants due for vaccines) and what is still owed.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Question stems describe a record or form and ask which FHSIS tool it is, or which statistic to compute from the data.\n- \"Which is the fundamental building block of FHSIS?\" Family Treatment Record. \"Which is used to track program clients?\" Target Client List. \"Which moves data upward?\" Reporting forms.\n- Trap: epidemic vs endemic. Compare to the expected level, not to absolute numbers.\n- Trap: descriptive epidemiology describes; it does not test why.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Vital Statistics]], [[Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions|DOH]], [[Epidemiology and Endemic Diseases]], [[Epidemiologic Triangle and Patterns of Disease Occurrence]], [[Communicable Disease Terminology]], [[Three Levels of Prevention]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Current FHSIS form names, reporting schedule and whether an electronic version replaced paper forms (verify local policy).\n- The monthly versus quarterly reporting split, and which forms carry which program data (verify local policy).\n- The endemic examples by province come from one handout; endemic areas change with control programs.",

"id": "np1-endemic-diseases",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Locally Endemic Diseases",
"body": "## What it is\n\"Locally endemic\" means a disease that is regularly present in an area. In the Philippines, reviewers commonly list tuberculosis, dengue, malaria, schistosomiasis, filariasis, leprosy, rabies, leptospirosis, soil-transmitted helminthiasis, diarrheal disease and others. Which ones are \"endemic\" depends on the province, so the nurse works from local data. Handout examples of endemic areas: schistosomiasis in Leyte and Samar, filariasis in Sorsogon, malaria in Palawan.\n\n## Must-know facts\nGeneral control logic for each (verify specifics locally):\n- Tuberculosis: Mycobacterium tuberculosis, airborne; incubation about 3 to 8 weeks; communicable as long as bacilli are discharged in sputum. Signs: cough of two weeks or more, afternoon rise of temperature, night sweats, weight loss, fatigue, hemoptysis. Sputum AFB is the confirmatory exam; chest X-ray and tuberculin (Mantoux) test support it. Treatment is six-month short-course chemotherapy (isoniazid, rifampicin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol) under DOTS (directly observed treatment, short course), the strategy that prevents non-compliance. Drug points: rifampicin turns body fluids red-orange and weakens oral contraceptives; isoniazid causes peripheral neuropathy (give vitamin B6, avoid alcohol); pyrazinamide raises uric acid and is hepatotoxic; ethambutol can cause optic neuritis (report visual changes, avoid in young children). Control: case finding, DOTS, BCG for infants, ventilation, cough etiquette, less overcrowding.\n- Dengue: virus (four serotypes) spread by the bite of the female Aedes aegypti, a day-biting mosquito that breeds in clean standing water; incubation 3 to 15 days. Signs: sudden high fever (39 to 40 C) for about 4 days, headache, pain behind the eyes, rash; then the toxic (hemorrhagic) stage when the temperature falls, with severe abdominal pain, vomiting, melena or hematemesis, and shock risk; then convalescence. Herman's sign (maculopapular rash with patches of normal skin) is the handout's pathognomonic sign. Tests: tourniquet test, falling platelets, hemoconcentration of 20% or more, NS1 antigen. Care: fluid replacement, oral rehydration, acetaminophen-type analgesic only (no aspirin), ice bag to the forehead and nose bridge for nosebleed, watch for shock. Control: search and destroy breeding sites, cover and clean containers, protect from bites, seek early consultation.\n- Malaria: Plasmodium species (falciparum is the most common and most dangerous), spread by the bite of the female Anopheles, a night-biting mosquito that prefers clear slow water. Signs: paroxysms of shaking chills, rapidly rising fever, headache, then profuse sweating; spleen and liver enlargement. Control: insecticide-treated nets, house spraying, stream clearing and seeding, long clothing in the evening, avoiding outdoor activity at night, prompt diagnosis and treatment; chloroquine prophylaxis (weekly, started 1 to 2 weeks before entering an endemic area) for travelers.\n- Schistosomiasis: Schistosoma japonicum, with the snail Oncomelania hupensis quadrasi as intermediate host; infection through skin contact with contaminated water. Stages: swimmer's itch about a day after penetration; Katayama fever (fever, chills, cough, enlarged liver, spleen, nodes) with bloody mucoid stools; chronic liver fibrosis, ascites, varices. Confirm by stool exam (fecalysis) and the circumoval precipitation test. Drug of choice praziquantel. Control: reduce snails (molluscicides, clearing vegetation), sanitary excreta disposal, footbridges over infested streams, safe water, control of stray animals, health teaching.\n- Filariasis: Wuchereria bancrofti and Brugia species spread by mosquitoes; incubation about 8 to 16 months. Stages: asymptomatic, acute (lymphangitis, lymphadenitis, epididymitis, orchitis), then chronic after 10 to 15 years (hydrocele, lymphedema, elephantiasis). Nocturnal blood examination at about 8 pm; the immunochromatographic card test can be done in the daytime. Treatment diethylcarbamazine (Hetrazan); control by annual mass drug administration, nets, repellents and hygiene.\n- Leprosy: Mycobacterium leprae, slow bacterial disease of skin and nerves, spread by prolonged close contact and droplets. Early signs: pale or reddish patch with loss of sensation, loss of sweating and hair over the patch, thickened painful nerves. Late: loss of eyebrows (madarosis), inability to close the eyelid (lagophthalmos), clawing of fingers, sinking of the nose bridge. Slit-skin smear confirms. Multiple drug therapy: multibacillary (infectious type) rifampicin, clofazimine, dapsone for about 12 months; paucibacillary rifampicin and dapsone for 6 to 9 months. Control: early MDT, contact tracing, protection of anesthetic limbs and eyes.\n- Rabies: Rhabdovirus in the saliva of an infected animal (usually a dog); incubation usually weeks but may be days to years, shorter for deep bites near the head. Phases: prodrome (fever, restlessness, apprehension), excitement (hydrophobia, aerophobia, drooling, spasms of throat muscles), then paralysis. Nearly always fatal once symptoms appear, so prevention matters: wash the bite wound immediately with soap and running water, apply antiseptic, go to an animal bite center for post-exposure vaccine, vaccinate dogs yearly from about 3 months of age, confine a biting dog for 10 to 14 days. The patient is kept in a quiet, dim room with no running water in sight.\n- Soil-transmitted worms (ascaris, hookworm, trichuris): spread through contaminated soil and poor sanitation; control by sanitary toilets, footwear, handwashing, regular deworming of schoolchildren and preschoolers.\n- Leptospirosis: bacteria from the urine of rats and other animals in floodwater; wading in flood causes exposure. See its own note.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Treat urgent signs first (severe dehydration, shock after the dengue fever drop, severe bleeding, airway problems, a fresh animal bite wound). Life threats before reporting and teaching (ABC, unstable before stable).\n2. Case finding and reporting to the rural health unit, because early reporting triggers community control.\n3. Isolate or counsel as appropriate (respiratory precautions for a sputum-positive TB client); follow the national treatment protocol.\n4. Contact tracing and community clean-up or vector control.\n5. Teach prevention and the importance of finishing treatment.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nClean surroundings, remove standing water, cover water containers, use nets or repellents as advised, wash hands, wash a bite wound immediately and seek care, wear footwear, take every dose of TB medicine until the health worker says to stop.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Matching disease to vector or reservoir (Aedes aegypti, Anopheles, Oncomelania snail, dog) and to the stage or sign (Herman's sign, Katayama fever, swimmer's itch, hydrophobia).\n- Choosing the \"most effective\" control: breaking the transmission link (vector breeding, sanitation) beats treating each case alone.\n- Trap: dengue's greatest danger is after the fever drops, not at the first fever.\n- Trap: dengue patients get no aspirin; TB patients on rifampicin get orange urine, which is expected.\n- Trap: the infectious leprosy type is the multibacillary (lepromatous) type.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Vital Statistics]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]], [[Water Supply and Toilet Facilities]], [[Tuberculosis: Features and Diagnosis]], [[Tuberculosis: Treatment and Nursing Care]], [[Dengue Fever]], [[Malaria]], [[Schistosomiasis]], [[Filariasis]], [[Leprosy (Hansen's Disease)]], [[Rabies]], [[Leptospirosis]], [[Epidemiologic Triangle and Patterns of Disease Occurrence]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Treatment regimens, drug names, program names, and which diseases are formally \"endemic\" now (verify local policy).\n- The outline does not list individual diseases under this heading; the list above comes from general knowledge and the communicable disease handout.\n- The handout calls the lepromatous type \"not infectious\" in one line and the multibacillary type \"infectious\" in another; the infectious type is multibacillary (lepromatous), so treat that handout line as an error.\n- Dengue: the handout names Aedes aegypti; some books also note Aedes albopictus. The dengue 4S slogan (search and destroy, self-protection, seek consultation, support fogging only in outbreaks) is standard DOH teaching, not from the handout (verify).\n- Time to wash a bite wound (commonly 10 to 15 minutes) and the animal-bite vaccine schedule (verify local policy).\n- Filariasis vector species and the single-dose mass drug combination used now (verify local policy).",

"id": "np1-vital-statistics",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Vital Statistics",
"body": "## What it is\nVital statistics are numbers on life events in a population: births, illnesses, marriages, divorces, separations and deaths. Morbidity (illness) and mortality (death) indicate the health of a community and the success or failure of health work. Nurses use them to describe community health, to compare places and years, and as the base for planning, implementing, monitoring and evaluating programs.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Sources of data: population census, registration of vital events, health surveys, studies and research.\n- A rate has a numerator (events), a denominator (the population at risk) and a multiplier (per 1,000, per 100,000, per 100). A ratio compares two quantities without regard to time or place, and the numerator is not part of the denominator.\n- Crude birth rate: live births divided by midyear population, times 1,000. It measures natural increase in one respect.\n- Crude death rate: deaths from all causes divided by midyear population, times 1,000. Natural increase equals birth rate minus death rate.\n- Infant mortality rate: infant deaths (under one year) divided by live births, times 1,000. It is a good index of the general health condition of a community.\n- Neonatal mortality: deaths in the first 28 days per 1,000 live births (the risk of dying in the first month).\n- Maternal mortality: maternal deaths divided by live births, times 100,000. It measures the risk of dying from causes related to pregnancy, childbirth and the puerperium and indexes the obstetric care received. The handout calls it a rate; because the denominator is live births it is strictly a ratio.\n- Fetal death: death of the product of conception before complete expulsion, regardless of duration of pregnancy; it measures pregnancy wastage (usually per 1,000 live births).\n- Attack rate: new cases during an outbreak among those exposed or at risk, times 100; a more accurate measure of risk of exposure.\n- Case fatality ratio: deaths from a disease divided by cases of that disease, times 100. It shows the \"killing power\" of a disease and is distorted by incomplete reporting.\n- Proportionate mortality uses deaths from one cause over all deaths.\n- Sex ratio: number of males divided by number of females, times 100.\n- Swaroop's index: deaths of persons 50 years or older divided by total deaths, times 100. A higher value suggests better health conditions, because more people reach old age.\n- Midyear population is used because the population changes during the year.\n- Worked example (made-up numbers): 40 infant deaths and 2,000 live births gives 20 per 1,000 live births.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Pick the correct formula from what the question asks (births, deaths, infants?).\n2. Check the denominator and the multiplier before computing.\n3. Interpret: compare with earlier years or other areas, not just a single number.\n4. Use trends to set priorities with the barangay and rural health unit.\n5. Make sure the events are registered; birth registration teaching is part of prenatal and postpartum care.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Vital statistics sources: civil registration records, health facility reports and surveys.\n- Natality = births, mortality = deaths, morbidity = illness.\n- Specific rates (age-specific, cause-specific) narrow the denominator to a group; crude rates use the whole population.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Computation stems: identify the denominator first. Trap: using total population when the formula needs live births.\n- Trap: Swaroop's index counts deaths of those 50 and above in the numerator, not the population.\n- \"Which is the best index of a community's general health?\" Infant mortality rate.\n- \"Which measures obstetric care?\" Maternal mortality.\n- Trap: case fatality uses cases as the denominator; attack rate uses the population at risk.\n\n## Memory aid\nFor Swaroop: \"Old deaths over all deaths.\" This is my own phrase.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid]], [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Epidemiology and Endemic Diseases]], [[Newborn Screening]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact multipliers and definitions (some texts differ on neonatal or fetal ratios) (verify local policy).\n- The attack-rate and case-fatality multipliers (per 100) and the sex-ratio multiplier are the common textbook forms; confirm your school's version.\n- Whether your textbook names maternal mortality a \"rate\" or a \"ratio\" (both appear).",

"rates",
"computation"

"id": "np1-incidence-prevalence-pyramid",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid",
"body": "## What it is\nIncidence and prevalence measure illness in a population. A population pyramid is a graph of age and sex groups that shows the shape of the population.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Incidence: NEW cases in a period among the population at risk. It measures the frequency of occurrence during a given time, and the risk of getting the disease.\n- Prevalence: ALL existing cases (old plus new) as a proportion of the population at a point or over a period. It measures how widespread a disease is.\n- Rough relationship: prevalence depends on incidence multiplied by the average duration of the disease. Prevalence rises when new cases rise, or when people live long with the disease (chronic illness, improving treatment); it falls with cure or death.\n- Incidence uses people who could still get the disease as the denominator; prevalence uses the whole population.\n- Formulas (common form): incidence rate = new cases in a period divided by population at risk, times a multiplier; prevalence rate = all cases at a time divided by total population, times a multiplier.\n- Population pyramid: age groups on the vertical axis, males on one side and females on the other.\n- A wide base and narrow top (an expanding pyramid) suggests high birth rate, high death rate and many young dependents. A narrow base means falling births (aging population). A roughly rectangular shape means stable low birth and death.\n- Dependency ratio: people too young (under 15) or too old (65 and above) compared with working-age people (15 to 64), times 100.\n- Attack rate: incidence during an outbreak among those exposed.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Decide whether the question asks about new cases (incidence) or all cases (prevalence).\n2. Read the pyramid shape to plan services: many children means immunization, growth monitoring and nutrition programs; many elderly means noncommunicable disease and geriatric services; many women of reproductive age means maternal and family planning services.\n3. Use incidence to judge whether prevention is working and prevalence to size the care load.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Point prevalence is at one moment; period prevalence is over time.\n- A falling incidence with rising prevalence can mean better survival.\n- Compare rates of the same kind: incidence with incidence, prevalence with prevalence.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which rate tells how many people newly developed...?\" The answer is incidence.\n- Trap: a better treatment that keeps people alive raises prevalence without raising incidence.\n- Interpret what a base-heavy pyramid implies for the community nurse's priorities.\n- Trap: a sudden outbreak increases incidence first; prevalence follows.\n- Trap: a lifelong, short-lived disease (a quick-killing illness) can have high incidence but low prevalence.\n\n## Memory aid\nIncidence = \"Incoming\" new cases; Prevalence = \"Pool\" of all cases. This is my own aid.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Vital Statistics]], [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Epidemiology and Endemic Diseases]], [[Epidemiologic Triangle and Patterns of Disease Occurrence]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Wording of your school's formulas and pyramid types (verify local policy).\n- The dependency-ratio age cut-offs (15 and 65 are common; some books use 14 and 65).",

"id": "np1-epi-overview",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Expanded Program on Immunization",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Expanded Program on Immunization (EPI) is the national program that protects children (and pregnant women) against vaccine-preventable diseases through routine and catch-up vaccination. Its goal is high coverage so that the whole community is protected. The EPI law is commonly cited as PD 996, and RA 10152 (2011) makes basic immunization of infants and children mandatory.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Herd (community) immunity: when enough people are immune, the disease cannot spread easily, which also protects those who cannot be vaccinated.\n- Routine immunization is delivered on scheduled days at health facilities, usually with outreach to remote areas.\n- Chikiting Ligtas is the Philippine name for mass or catch-up immunization campaigns (for example measles and polio rounds) that supplement routine service. It reaches children missed by routine visits.\n- EPI principles from the handout:\n  - It is safe and effective to give all EPI vaccines on the same day at different body sites.\n  - Measles vaccine is given as soon as the child is 9 months old; in an endemic area it may be given as early as 6 months. Protection is about 85% if given at 9 months and about 95% at one year or older.\n  - The schedule is not restarted; a missed dose is simply given. No extra doses are given to a child or mother who missed a dose of DPT, hepatitis B, OPV or TT.\n  - Giving doses closer together than the recommended interval may lessen the antibody response.\n  - Freeze-dried vaccines are reconstituted only with the diluent supplied for that vaccine.\n  - One syringe and one needle per child.\n- Handout routine schedule (minimum age at first dose, doses, interval): BCG at birth, 1 dose; pentavalent from 6 weeks, 3 doses, 4 weeks apart; OPV from 6 weeks, 3 doses, 4 weeks apart; hepatitis B at birth, then 6 weeks after the first dose; measles at 9 months, 1 dose; MMR at 12 months, 1 dose; IPV at 14 weeks, 1 dose.\n- False contraindications (do vaccinate): malnutrition, low-grade fever, mild respiratory infection, cough, diarrhea, vomiting.\n- Absolute contraindications: DPT 2 or 3 in a child who had convulsions or shock within 3 days after the previous dose; whole-cell pertussis-containing vaccine in a child with neurologic disease; live vaccines such as BCG in someone immunosuppressed (for example by malignant disease).\n- Target setting: the number of eligible children is estimated from the total population using a percentage for infants (and another for pregnant women) from your textbook, then divided across months or sessions to plan vaccines and supplies.\n- A child is counted as \"fully immunized\" when all the vaccines in the national definition are completed within the age limit (commonly BCG, 3 pentavalent, 3 OPV and measles before the first birthday).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Check each child's immunization card and screen for contraindications before vaccinating. Safety comes first.\n2. Give the vaccine correctly (right vaccine, route, site, dose, interval).\n3. Record in the child's card and the register (and the target client list).\n4. Teach the caregiver what to expect and when to return.\n5. Track defaulters and follow up at home.\n6. Plan supplies from the target population, with a buffer for wastage and a working cold chain.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Defaulter: child who missed a scheduled dose; follow up and give the missed dose without restarting the series.\n- Coverage: vaccinated divided by target, times 100.\n- Dropout rate: those who got the first dose but not the later ones (for example first pentavalent minus third pentavalent, over the first).\n- Missed opportunity: a child comes to the facility and leaves without needed vaccines because of a false contraindication.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nKeep the vaccination card; return on the scheduled date; mild fever or soreness is common; bring the child even if mildly ill with a cold (mild illness alone is usually not a reason to postpone), but report severe reactions.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Questions on the elements of the program, definitions, coverage computation and which activity addresses a low-coverage barangay.\n- Trap: a vaccine missed does not require restarting the series; resume where it left off.\n- Trap: a malnourished child with diarrhea is still vaccinated; a child who convulsed within 3 days of DPT is not given the next DPT.\n- Measles timing and protection percentages are frequent single-fact items.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Cold Chain and Vaccine Logistics]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]], [[BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine]], [[Tetanus Toxoid Immunization]], [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Measles (Rubeola)]], [[Poliomyelitis]], [[Types of Immunity and Antigens]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The current schedule, the list of vaccines in the program (the national schedule now includes more vaccines than the handout), the fully immunized child definition and target percentages (verify local policy).\n- The handout lists the pentavalent at 6 weeks with 4-week intervals; check it against the ages your school teaches (6, 10 and 14 weeks).\n- Whether the 6-month early measles dose in endemic areas still applies.\n- The handout cites the EPI law as PD 996 and RA 10152; verify the numbers and what each requires.",

"id": "np1-cold-chain",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Cold Chain and Vaccine Logistics",
"body": "## What it is\nThe cold chain is the system that keeps vaccines at the correct temperature from manufacturer to the child. A broken cold chain makes vaccines lose potency without any visible change, and a vaccine that has lost potency gives a child no protection while looking normal.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Vaccines are sensitive to heat, and some (the adsorbed ones such as pentavalent, hepatitis B, tetanus toxoid and IPV) are damaged by freezing.\n- Typical principle: most vaccines are stored in a refrigerator at about 2 to 8 degrees Celsius. Freeze-dried vaccines (BCG, measles, MMR) and oral polio can tolerate or are kept frozen at the supply (regional or provincial) level, but at the health center they sit in the refrigerator. Check your textbook for which vaccines are kept in which compartment.\n- Heat sensitivity order often taught: OPV is the most heat-sensitive; measles and BCG are also sensitive; toxoids are the most stable but still freeze-sensitive.\n- Light sensitivity: BCG and measles (freeze-dried, reconstituted) are damaged by light; keep them in their box or carrier.\n- Equipment at the health center level: a vaccine refrigerator (or ice-lined unit), a thermometer, cold boxes and vaccine carriers with frozen ice packs for outreach.\n- The temperature is checked and recorded at least twice a day (morning and afternoon), including weekends where required, to catch a failure early.\n- Vaccine vial monitor (VVM): a square within a circle on the label that darkens with accumulated heat. If the inner square is lighter than the circle, the vaccine can be used; if it matches or is darker than the circle, discard. The VVM does not show freezing damage.\n- Shake test: used on suspected frozen adsorbed vaccines. A frozen-then-thawed vial settles quickly into a clear liquid with sediment, compared with a never-frozen control vial that stays cloudy longer; if it settles faster, discard.\n- \"First expiry, first out\" stock management: use the vials that expire first.\n- Open-vial rules depend on the vaccine. Reconstituted freeze-dried vaccines (BCG, measles) must be kept cold and shaded and are discarded after about six hours or at the end of the session. Some liquid multi-dose vials can be kept for a longer period under the open-vial policy if handled and stored correctly.\n- Arranging the refrigerator: keep vials on shelves with space for air to circulate; never in the door; never against the cooling plate; diluents stored cool but not frozen; nothing else (food, drinks, specimens) inside.\n- Power failure: keep the door closed, do not open it unnecessarily, note the time, and move the vaccines to cold boxes with frozen ice packs if the outage goes on.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Keep the cold chain unbroken: the first action on finding a temperature problem is to protect the vaccines (move them to a working cold box) and report, before deciding about use.\n2. Check and record temperatures routinely.\n3. Do not store food, drinks or other items in the vaccine refrigerator.\n4. Arrange vials so air can circulate; place the most freeze-sensitive vaccines away from the cooling element.\n5. Do not use a vaccine that has been exposed to heat or freezing until the supervisor decides it is safe.\n6. Take inventory and request supplies on a schedule to avoid stock-outs.\n7. For outreach, pack the carrier with frozen ice packs, put a foam pad on top of the vaccines, close the lid tightly, keep it in the shade, and return unopened vials to the refrigerator.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Logistics management: forecast, order, receive, store, distribute, and monitor supplies.\n- Wastage: unused doses discarded; multi-dose vials affect planning.\n- Conditioned ice packs: frozen packs allowed to start to thaw slightly (water droplets form) before packing, so they do not freeze the vaccines by contact.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Where in the refrigerator?\" or \"What to do when power fails?\" Think: protect the vaccine first.\n- Trap: freezing damages some vaccines even though they look normal; cold is not always safe.\n- Trap: a VVM that is darker than the circle means discard even if the expiry date has not passed.\n- Trap: the shake test detects freezing; the VVM detects heat.\n\n## Memory aid\n\"Cold, dark, not frozen (except some), and first-to-expire out.\" This is my own summary.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]], [[BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine]], [[Types of Immunity and Antigens]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The exact temperature ranges, shelf placement per vaccine, how long vaccines last in a carrier and the open-vial policy (verify local policy).\n- The handout material for this note was thin; the VVM, shake test and open-vial points come from standard immunization training, not from the handout.\n- Which vaccines the Philippine program lets you freeze at each level (verify local policy).",

"id": "np1-vaccine-principles",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities",
"body": "## What it is\nGeneral rules that apply to every vaccine, plus a quick comparison of the main childhood vaccines. This is the \"table\" idea: for each vaccine know its type (live or inactivated), dose, route, site, and key precaution.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Live attenuated vaccines (examples: BCG, measles, MMR, oral polio, rotavirus) use weakened organisms. One dose often gives long-lasting immunity. They are generally avoided in people who are severely immunocompromised (for example malignancy) and in pregnancy.\n- Inactivated or toxoid vaccines (examples: tetanus toxoid, diphtheria and pertussis components, hepatitis B, inactivated polio) cannot cause the disease, but protection is shorter and multiple doses and boosters are needed.\n- Handout table of dose, route and site:\n  - BCG: 0.05 mL intradermal, right deltoid region.\n  - DPT or pentavalent: 0.5 mL intramuscular, upper outer thigh (vastus lateralis).\n  - OPV: 2 to 3 drops by mouth.\n  - Hepatitis B: 0.5 mL intramuscular, upper outer thigh.\n  - Measles: 0.5 mL subcutaneous, outer upper arm.\n- Route and site matter: intradermal, subcutaneous, intramuscular or oral. Giving the wrong route reduces effect or increases reaction. Infants usually receive intramuscular injections in the front-outer thigh (vastus lateralis).\n- Contraindication principle: a severe allergic reaction to a previous dose or to a component is a true reason not to give that vaccine again. Mild illness, low-grade fever, cough, mild diarrhea, vomiting or malnutrition are NOT reasons to delay.\n- Absolute contraindications in the handout: convulsions or shock within 3 days of a previous DPT dose; neurologic disease with whole-cell pertussis vaccine; live vaccines such as BCG in someone immunosuppressed by malignant disease.\n- Common mild reactions: soreness, redness, low fever, irritability. Severe allergic reaction (anaphylaxis) is rare but needs emergency action.\n- Several vaccines can be given on the same day at separate sites with separate syringes.\n- Never mix vaccines in one syringe; reconstitute freeze-dried vaccines only with the diluent that comes with them.\n- Use a new sterile syringe and needle for each child; follow safe disposal in a sharps container without recapping.\n- Missed doses: continue the series; do not restart. Extra doses are not given to make up for a missed dose of DPT, hepatitis B, OPV or TT; doses given too close together may blunt the response.\n- Types of immunity: natural active (after illness), natural passive (placental antibodies), artificial active (vaccine or toxoid), artificial passive (antitoxin, immunoglobulin).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Screen: ask about prior severe reactions, current illness, and special conditions. Safety before giving.\n2. Check the vaccine (name, expiry, appearance, vaccine vial monitor) and prepare it correctly.\n3. Explain to the caregiver and obtain consent.\n4. Give with correct technique, route and site; keep epinephrine and emergency supplies available.\n5. Observe the child for a short time afterwards for immediate reactions.\n6. Document and teach about expected effects and when to seek help.\n7. Report serious adverse events following immunization to the proper office.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Adverse event following immunization (AEFI): any untoward medical event after a vaccine, whether or not caused by it.\n- Anaphylaxis: airway swelling, breathing difficulty, drop in blood pressure; first action is call for help and give emergency treatment (epinephrine per protocol) while keeping the airway open.\n- Vaccine reaction types: vaccine product reaction, program error (wrong site, non-sterile technique), coincidental event, anxiety-related reaction.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which child should NOT be vaccinated today?\" Look for a history of a severe reaction to the same vaccine, or convulsions after the last DPT, not for a runny nose.\n- \"Which vaccine is live?\" Know the list: BCG, OPV, measles, MMR, rotavirus.\n- \"What route and site for measles?\" Subcutaneous, outer upper arm.\n- Trap: pregnancy and live vaccines.\n- Trap: abscess after injection points to non-sterile technique (a program error), not the vaccine itself.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Cold Chain and Vaccine Logistics]], [[BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine]], [[Tetanus Toxoid Immunization]], [[Types of Immunity and Antigens]], [[Measles (Rubeola)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Which vaccines are in the current Philippine schedule (for example rotavirus, pneumococcal, HPV, measles-rubella variations), their ages, doses, and sites (verify local policy). The handout table covers only the older core vaccines.\n- How long to observe after vaccination (commonly about 15 to 30 minutes) and the AEFI reporting route (verify local policy).\n- The handout lists measles as subcutaneous; check whether MMR or MR products in your textbook use the same route.",

"id": "np1-bcg-pentavalent",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine",
"body": "## What it is\nBCG protects against severe forms of tuberculosis in young children. The pentavalent vaccine combines protection against five diseases in one injection: diphtheria, pertussis, tetanus, hepatitis B and Haemophilus influenzae type b.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- BCG is a live vaccine given into the skin (intradermal), 0.05 mL in the right deltoid region per the handout. It is given at birth (the schedule's minimum age is birth). Verify the policy for age limits.\n- A correct injection raises a small wheal (blister-like bump) in the skin.\n- Expected BCG reaction: a small papule after a few weeks, which may become a pustule, ulcerate and heal with a scar over a few months. This is normal and does not need dressings, ointment or antibiotics.\n- BCG is avoided in severe immunocompromise (for example symptomatic HIV, or malignant disease per the handout) and in children with generalized skin disease.\n- BCG gives partial protection: it helps prevent severe childhood TB like meningitis and miliary TB, not all adult lung TB. The country TB control plan still relies on finding and treating sputum-positive cases.\n- Pentavalent: DPT plus hepatitis B plus Hib. It is given intramuscularly, 0.5 mL, in the front-outer (vastus lateralis) thigh of the infant, three doses starting at 6 weeks with at least 4 weeks between doses per the handout.\n- Hepatitis B vaccine is also given at birth as a separate dose; the handout shows a further dose 6 weeks after it.\n- Mild reactions: fever, local pain and swelling, irritability. A fever-reducing measure may be advised per local policy.\n- Severe reactions to a previous dose (high continuous crying, convulsions, collapse, encephalopathy) are reasons to withhold further pertussis-containing doses. Per the handout, DPT 2 or 3 is not given if convulsions or shock occurred within 3 days of the last dose, and whole-cell pertussis is avoided in neurologic disease.\n- Diseases covered by the five parts: diphtheria (throat membrane), pertussis (whooping cough), tetanus, hepatitis B (liver) and Hib (pneumonia and meningitis in young children).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Screen for contraindications and previous reactions.\n2. Prepare and give the vaccine with the correct route: BCG intradermal (a wheal forms), pentavalent intramuscular.\n3. Do not massage the BCG site or cover with unneeded dressing; keep it clean and dry. For pentavalent, apply light pressure only if needed.\n4. Teach expected reactions and warning signs (spreading redness, high fever, convulsion).\n5. Record and schedule the next dose.\n6. Report and refer unusual reactions.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- A wheal at the BCG site means the injection went into the skin layer. No wheal means the dose went too deep or leaked; do not repeat on the same day without instruction.\n- If a deep, painful abscess or enlarged nearby lymph node develops, refer.\n- BCG scar check: a scar later shows the vaccine \"took\"; absence of a scar does not by itself require revaccination (verify local policy).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Route and site questions; normal BCG reaction vs a complication.\n- \"Which diseases does pentavalent protect against?\" Name all five.\n- Trap: a small ulcer and scar after BCG is expected, not an infection requiring antibiotics.\n- Trap: BCG goes into the skin; pentavalent goes into the muscle of the thigh.\n- Trap: the pentavalent is not a single-disease vaccine, so a child who had pertussis-type reactions is handled differently from one with a mild fever.\n\n## Memory aid\nPentavalent = penta = five diseases (D, P, T, HepB, Hib). Standard idea, not my invention.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Diphtheria]], [[Pertussis (Whooping Cough)]], [[Tuberculosis: Features and Diagnosis]], [[Cold Chain and Vaccine Logistics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Dose volumes, exact ages and intervals, site (arm side for BCG), and which pentavalent product the program currently uses (verify local policy).\n- The handout gives a different dose pattern for hepatitis B than newer programs; verify the birth-dose timing and the number of pentavalent doses.\n- The handout says \"no extra dose is given for a missed dose of DPT or hepatitis B\"; check whether your textbook adds catch-up rules.",

"title": "Maternal and Child Health",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Maternal and Child Health (MCH) program aims to reduce illness and death in mothers, newborns and children through care before, during and after pregnancy, and through child health services at community level. Under the family health programs, the child-health goal covers ages 0 to 9 years.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Prenatal care in the community: registration early in pregnancy, regular visits, weight and blood pressure checks, urine tests, abdominal exam, iron and folic acid supplements, tetanus protection, and teaching on diet, rest and danger signs.\n- Handout prenatal visit pattern: first visit as early as possible; second during the second trimester; third during the third trimester; then every 2 weeks after the eighth month until delivery. Newer guidance asks for more contacts, so verify.\n- Handout supplements: vitamin A 10,000 IU twice a week from the fourth month of pregnancy; iron and folic acid (60 mg and 400 micrograms) daily from the fifth month until two months after delivery. The handout also lists tetanus toxoid, treatment of conditions, clean and safe delivery, birth registration, newborn screening, breastfeeding and family planning counseling.\n- Postpartum visits in the handout: first within the first week, preferably days 3 to 5; second at 6 weeks.\n- Proper birth spacing: 3 to 5 years.\n- Danger signs in pregnancy that need immediate referral: vaginal bleeding, severe headache with blurred vision, convulsions, swelling of face and hands, severe abdominal pain, fever, decreased fetal movement, leaking fluid.\n- Birth planning: skilled attendant, health facility delivery, transport and emergency fund arrangements, and a blood donor if needed.\n- Postpartum care: early visits, check bleeding, uterus, temperature, wound healing, breastfeeding, mood, and family planning counseling.\n- Breastfeeding (IYCF key messages): start within the first hour, exclusive for the first six months (no water, no other liquids or solids), on demand, with proper attachment, complementary foods at six months, and continue to two years and beyond. Mother-baby friendly practices (rooming-in, no pacifiers or bottles) support it.\n- Child health programs in the handout: infant and young child feeding, newborn screening, EPI, management of childhood illnesses, micronutrient supplementation, dental health, early child development, injuries.\n- Child health basics: growth monitoring (weight and length), immunization, vitamin A, deworming, ORS for diarrhea, and care for pneumonia and fever.\n- IMCI (Integrated Management of Childhood Illness): assess, classify, then treat, using color-coded categories (pink: urgent referral; yellow: treatment and advice; green: home care). General danger signs in a sick child age 2 months to 5 years: unable to drink or breastfeed, vomits everything, convulsions, lethargy or unconscious. The main symptoms checked: cough or difficult breathing, diarrhea, fever, ear problem, plus nutrition, anemia and immunization status.\n- IMCI quick clues (standard): fast breathing counts (60 or more for under 2 months, 50 or more for 2 to 11 months, 40 or more for 1 to 5 years) point to pneumonia; chest indrawing or stridor in a calm child is severe pneumonia; dehydration signs (restless, sunken eyes, thirsty, skin pinch slow) guide ORS plans.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify danger signs first; refer urgently before routine tasks (ABC and unstable before stable).\n2. Assess the mother or child fully (history, vital signs, growth).\n3. Treat or give supplements per protocol.\n4. Teach the caregiver the key actions at home.\n5. Follow up and record.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Risk approach: identify high-risk mothers (very young, older, many births, short spacing, prior complications) for more frequent care.\n- Safe motherhood: pregnancy, delivery and postpartum care that is safe.\n- Clean and safe delivery: skilled attendant, clean hands and surface, clean cord care.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nEat varied foods, take iron-folic acid, deliver with a skilled attendant, breastfeed exclusively, complete the child's vaccines, use ORS and continue feeding in diarrhea, return at once for danger signs.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which finding needs immediate referral or action? Pick the life-threatening danger sign (convulsion, heavy bleeding).\n- IMCI questions: first decide if a general danger sign is present; if yes, urgent referral follows.\n- \"Postpartum visit timing\" and \"birth spacing\" are common fact items.\n- Trap: exclusive breastfeeding means no water either.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Reproductive Health and Family Planning]], [[Tetanus Toxoid Immunization]], [[EINC and Active Management of Third Stage of Labor]], [[BEmOC and CEmOC]], [[Newborn Screening]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Nutrition]], [[Maternal Health Program: Prenatal Visits, Supplements and Postpartum Care]], [[Infant and Young Child Feeding]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The minimum prenatal visit schedule (the handout pattern is older; newer DOH guidance asks for at least four visits with set timing), the exact IMCI color categories and treatment plans, and the details of the breastfeeding law (verify local policy).\n- The handout vitamin A schedule in pregnancy and the iron-folic acid timing: vitamin A in pregnancy is handled cautiously in newer guidance, and iron-folic acid is now given earlier. Treat these numbers as doubtful.\n- The IMCI breathing-rate cut-offs (verify against the current IMCI chart).",

"id": "np1-reproductive-health-fp",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Reproductive Health and Family Planning",
"body": "## What it is\nReproductive health is complete physical, mental and social well-being in all matters of the reproductive system. In the Philippines, services are guided by a national Responsible Parenthood and Reproductive Health law (commonly cited as RA 10354; verify its details). Family planning (FP) aims for universal access to FP information and services, to help couples reach their desired family size within responsible parenthood and to reduce infant, neonatal, under-five and maternal deaths.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Counseling must be voluntary and informed, respecting the couple's choice and religion.\n- Reproductive health elements (handout): family planning; maternal and child health and nutrition; prevention and management of reproductive tract infections; adolescent reproductive health; prevention and management of abortion and its complications; breast and reproductive tract cancers and other gynecological conditions; education and counseling on sexuality; men's reproductive health and involvement; violence against women and children; infertility and sexual dysfunction. Main objectives: reduce maternal and child mortality, halt and reverse the spread of HIV/AIDS, and widen access to information and services.\n- Permanent methods: female sterilization (bilateral tubal ligation; cuts or blocks both tubes; no effect on breastfeeding; rare surgical risks; reversal is difficult; no protection from infections) and male sterilization (vasectomy; vas deferens tied and cut; effective after about 3 months, so another method is used meanwhile; pain and swelling for 2 to 3 days; sexual ability and ejaculation are not lost). Both need informed consent.\n- Pill (estrogen and progesterone): convenient and reduces painful periods; needs consistent daily use; side effects include nausea, dizziness, breast tenderness; can suppress lactation.\n- Condom: latex sheath that blocks sperm and infections; no hormones; may cause allergy in latex-sensitive persons; encourages male participation. Dual protection means a condom with another method.\n- Injectable: progestin that suppresses ovulation, thickens cervical mucus and changes the uterine lining; reversible, no daily intake, no estrogen effects.\n- Intrauterine device: inserted by trained provider; teach the string check and danger signs.\n- Lactational amenorrhea method (LAM): works when the baby is under six months, the mother is fully or nearly fully breastfeeding and her menses have not returned. If one condition fails, advise another method. Effectiveness drops if mother and baby are separated for long.\n- Natural (fertility awareness) methods: cervical mucus (Billings), basal body temperature (a small dip before ovulation and a rise of about one degree Fahrenheit after it), symptothermal (combines mucus, temperature and other signs), Two Day Method (check for secretions daily), Standard Days Method (color-coded cycle beads; fertile days 8 to 19 of the cycle), calendar (rhythm). These need abstinence on fertile days and cooperation of the partner.\n- Common misconceptions to correct: FP causes abortion, makes couples sterile, or kills sexual desire.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the client's needs, history, medical conditions and wishes before suggesting a method.\n2. Give complete information on options, effectiveness and side effects.\n3. Let the client choose; support their decision.\n4. Teach correct use and warning signs.\n5. Provide follow-up and referral for conditions that prohibit a method.\n6. Ensure FP supplies and logistics are available (a PHN role along with counseling and the service package).\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Effectiveness: perfect use vs typical use.\n- Dual protection: a condom with another method.\n- Responsible parenthood: couples decide the number and spacing of children knowing their means and values.\n- Birth spacing: 3 to 5 years between births is the handout's recommendation.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nInvolve both partners, correct myths, and tell them where to get services and what to do for missed pills or problems.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which method fits a described client (breastfeeding, smoker, wants natural method)?\n- Trap: do not pick a method for the client; counseling respects choice.\n- Know the signs for which a client should return (for example IUD string missing, severe abdominal pain).\n- Trap: a mother with returned menses or one who adds formula no longer meets LAM criteria.\n- Trap: a condom is the only listed method that also protects against infections.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Maternal and Child Health]], [[VAWC and Violence Against Women and Children]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Family Planning Methods: Permanent, Hormonal and Barrier]], [[Fertility Awareness Family Planning Methods]], [[Maternal Health Program: Prenatal Visits, Supplements and Postpartum Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Which methods the DOH currently provides and their rules (verify local policy). Pill and injectable names, doses and schedules are not given here.\n- The handout says the Standard Days Method \"cannot be used by women who usually have cycles between 26 and 32 days.\" This is reversed: the method is meant for women with cycles of 26 to 32 days. Treat that line as an error.\n- The basal body temperature changes in the handout (a fall of 0.5 F before ovulation, a rise of 1.0 F during) are approximate; verify your textbook's numbers.\n- Fertile days of the Standard Days Method (8 to 19) are standard teaching, not in the handout.",

"id": "np1-tetanus-toxoid",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Tetanus Toxoid Immunization",
"body": "## What it is\nTetanus toxoid (TT) is given to women of childbearing age, especially during pregnancy, to protect the mother and the newborn from tetanus. Neonatal tetanus is caused by bacterial spores (Clostridium tetani, found in soil and feces) entering the umbilical stump, usually after unclean cord care.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- The toxoid is inactivated, so it is safe in pregnancy.\n- Handout schedule (dose, interval, protection, duration):\n  - TT1: as early as possible in pregnancy; no protection yet.\n  - TT2: at least 4 weeks after TT1; about 80% protection; lasts about 3 years.\n  - TT3: 6 months after TT2; about 95%; lasts about 5 years.\n  - TT4: 1 year after TT3; about 99%; lasts about 10 years.\n  - TT5: 1 year after TT4; about 99%; lifetime protection.\n- Antibodies cross the placenta, so a mother who is protected passes protection to the baby (natural passive immunity for the newborn, artificial active immunity for the mother).\n- It is given intramuscularly, usually in the upper arm.\n- Clean delivery and cord care are also essential: clean hands, clean cutting instrument, clean cord tie, and no harmful substances on the cord.\n- Neonatal tetanus: a baby who suckled normally for the first days stops sucking, develops stiffness and spasms (trismus, arched back), fever may be present. Typical onset is between the 3rd and 28th day of life.\n- Tetanus disease in older persons: incubation 3 to 21 days; lockjaw (trismus), risus sardonicus, board-like abdomen; treated with tetanus immune globulin (TIG), metronidazole or penicillin, sedatives, a quiet dim room and airway care. Adults keep protection with a booster every 10 years.\n- Some programs have moved from TT to a combined tetanus and diphtheria vaccine; verify local policy.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Check the woman's immunization status and card first. This decides which dose she needs.\n2. Screen for contraindications (severe reaction to a previous dose).\n3. Give the dose correctly and record on the TT card and the maternal record.\n4. Teach side effects (arm soreness, mild fever) and the next visit.\n5. Teach clean cord care and clean delivery.\n6. For a wound in an unprotected person, think of both the toxoid and, in a dirty wound, immune globulin per protocol.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- A woman fully protected earlier may need fewer new doses; check the card.\n- Assess a new pregnancy against the prior doses given.\n- \"Fully protected\" in maternal programs commonly means at least TT3 or the equivalent, such as completing the childhood series plus boosters (verify your school's definition).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Questions pair \"which dose number\" with the interval or protection given: TT2 gives 80% for 3 years, TT3 gives 95% for 5 years, TT4 and TT5 give 99% (10 years and lifetime).\n- Trap: do not restart the series if a dose was late.\n- Trap: the spacing is 4 weeks, 6 months, 1 year, 1 year; \"1 year\" appears twice.\n- Trap: neonatal tetanus prevention depends on both maternal immunization and clean cord care.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]], [[EINC and Active Management of Third Stage of Labor]], [[Tetanus (Lockjaw) Disease]], [[Types of Immunity and Antigens]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The dose intervals, the percentage of protection at each dose, and whether Td replaces TT locally (verify local policy).\n- The neonatal tetanus onset window (days 3 to 28) is standard teaching, not from the handout.\n- Whether TIG is given along with the toxoid for dirty wounds, and the booster rule for adults (every 10 years in the handout).",
