"id": "np1-einc-amtsl",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "EINC and Active Management of Third Stage of Labor",
"body": "## What it is\nEINC (Essential Intrapartum and Newborn Care) is a set of evidence-based practices for the time of birth and the first hours of the newborn. AMTSL (Active Management of the Third Stage of Labor) is a set of steps to deliver the placenta safely and prevent postpartum hemorrhage.\n\n## Must-know facts\nEINC core practices, in order for a well newborn (verify details in current DOH materials):\n- Immediate and thorough drying right after birth (about 30 seconds); this also stimulates breathing and prevents hypothermia. Do not wipe off the white coating (vernix) as part of routine. Remove wet cloth.\n- Skin-to-skin contact of the baby with the mother's chest, covered with a dry blanket and cap, kept in place until after the first breastfeed (commonly at least 90 minutes).\n- Properly timed cord clamping, not immediately: when pulsation stops or after a short delay (the obstetric handout says when pulsation stops or after 2 minutes; DOH teaching often says 1 to 3 minutes).\n- Non-separation of mother and baby for early and exclusive breastfeeding, with the first breastfeed within the first hour (60 minutes).\n- Then routine newborn care such as eye care, vitamin K, hepatitis B and BCG vaccines per policy, delayed until after the skin-to-skin period; weighing also waits.\n- Bathing is delayed for about six hours or more.\n- The sequence protects warmth, bonding, breastfeeding and iron stores.\n\nAMTSL typical components:\n- A uterotonic (oxytocin is the usual one, by injection, commonly 10 units IM) given within about a minute after the baby is born, after checking that there is no second baby.\n- Controlled cord traction with counter-pressure on the uterus above the pubic bone, during a contraction.\n- Uterine massage after the placenta comes out, then teach the mother to check her own uterus.\n\nThird stage facts from the obstetric handout: lasts about 5 to 10 minutes; up to 30 minutes is the maximum wait; beyond that is abnormal. Signs of placental separation: Calkin's sign (uterus becomes firm and globular), lengthening of the cord, sudden gush of blood, uterus rising in the abdomen. Schultze expulsion (shiny fetal surface first, less bleeding) versus Duncan (raw maternal surface first, more bleeding). Inspect the placenta for completeness (cotyledons) and three cord vessels (two arteries, one vein).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. If the newborn is not breathing or crying, stop routine steps and start resuscitation. Airway and breathing come first (ABC).\n2. For a well baby, do the EINC sequence in order, because the order protects warmth, bonding and breastfeeding.\n3. Do AMTSL steps in the correct order; check the placenta is complete and uterus is firm.\n4. Monitor bleeding, uterine firmness and vital signs for the first hours.\n5. Record and teach the mother about breastfeeding and cord care.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Fundus firm and at or below the umbilicus is the goal; a soft uterus is massaged and bleeding is reported.\n- Retained placenta or heavy bleeding is an emergency and calls for referral.\n- Kangaroo mother care: continuous skin-to-skin contact for small or preterm babies.\n- Rooming-in: mother and baby stay together 24 hours.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Sequence questions: which comes first after birth? (drying and skin-to-skin come before routine injections and weighing).\n- AMTSL: oxytocin first, then controlled traction, then massage.\n- Trap: skipping drying or separating a well baby for routine procedures goes against EINC.\n- Trap: pulling on the cord without counter-traction on the uterus risks uterine inversion.\n\n## Memory aid\nDry, skin, clamp late, feed. This is my own short summary.\n\n## Related topics\n[[BEmOC and CEmOC]], [[Newborn Screening]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Third Stage of Labor: Placental Expulsion and Uterotonic Drugs]], [[Fourth Stage of Labor and Postpartum Checks]], [[Newborn Vital Signs and Heat Conservation]], [[Postpartum Hemorrhage]], [[Laws Protecting Infant Feeding and Food Fortification]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Timing of cord clamping, timing of vitamin K and vaccine, and dose or route of oxytocin (verify local policy). The oxytocin dose and the 90-minute skin-to-skin period are standard teaching, not from the handout.\n- The handout's cord clamping rule (\"pulsation stops or 2 minutes\") differs slightly from 1 to 3 minutes taught in some DOH materials.\n- Exact bath delay (hours) varies between sources.",

"id": "np1-bemoc-cemoc",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "BEmOC and CEmOC",
"body": "## What it is\nEmergency obstetric and newborn care is organized into two levels. BEmOC (Basic Emergency Obstetric and Newborn Care) covers life-saving services that a well-equipped health center can provide. CEmOC (Comprehensive Emergency Obstetric and Newborn Care) includes everything in BEmOC plus surgery and blood transfusion at a hospital.\n\n## Must-know facts\nBEmOC signal functions (general list; verify the current version):\n- Give parenteral antibiotics\n- Give a parenteral uterotonic (oxytocin) drug\n- Give a parenteral anticonvulsant (magnesium sulfate) for pre-eclampsia or eclampsia\n- Manual removal of the placenta\n- Removal of retained products of conception\n- Assisted vaginal delivery (vacuum or forceps)\n- Basic newborn resuscitation\n\nCEmOC adds:\n- Cesarean section\n- Blood transfusion\n- (Care for sick and low-birth-weight newborns is also expected at CEmOC level.)\n\n- A BEmOC facility needs trained staff available around the clock and a referral link to a CEmOC facility.\n- Network planning standard commonly cited: for every 500,000 population, about four BEmOC facilities and one CEmOC facility.\n- Common causes of maternal death worldwide: hemorrhage, hypertensive disorders, sepsis, obstructed labor, unsafe abortion. These explain the signal functions.\n- Match each cause to its function: bleeding = uterotonic, manual removal, removal of retained products, transfusion; eclampsia = magnesium sulfate; infection = antibiotics; obstructed labor = assisted delivery or cesarean; baby not breathing = newborn resuscitation.\n- Three delays that kill: delay in deciding to seek care, delay reaching the facility, delay in receiving care at the facility.\n- Magnesium sulfate watch points (from the obstetric notes): check respiratory rate, deep tendon reflexes and urine output before each dose; the antidote is calcium gluconate.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Recognize the emergency early (heavy bleeding, convulsions, fever, prolonged labor).\n2. Start stabilizing measures within scope: call for help, position, IV access, oxygen, give the ordered or protocol-based drug. For a convulsing woman, protect the airway and prevent injury first, then the drug.\n3. Refer with a clear message, and send the client with a trained escort if possible.\n4. Communicate with the receiving facility so they prepare.\n5. Document and follow up.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Network of facilities: the barangay health station, rural health unit, BEmOC facility and CEmOC hospital form a referral chain.\n- Signal function: a key life-saving service used to classify a facility as BEmOC or CEmOC.\n- Two-way referral: the client is sent up for emergency care and referred back for follow-up.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- \"Which service belongs to CEmOC but not BEmOC?\" The answer is surgery and blood transfusion.\n- Trap: magnesium sulfate for seizures belongs to basic care.\n- Trap: manual removal of the placenta is a basic function, so a health center with a trained provider performs it.\n- Trap: assisted vaginal delivery (vacuum) is basic; cesarean is comprehensive.\n\n## Memory aid\nCEmOC = BEmOC + Cut (cesarean) + Blood. This is my own aid.\n\n## Related topics\n[[EINC and Active Management of Third Stage of Labor]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions|DOH]], [[Magnesium Sulfate and Eclampsia Care]], [[Postpartum Hemorrhage]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]], [[Pregnancy-Induced Hypertension: Classification and Pre-eclampsia]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Current signal function list and the standards for facilities (verify local policy).\n- The handout set had no BEmOC or CEmOC material; the list and the 4:1 facility ratio come from standard emergency obstetric care teaching.\n- Whether newborn resuscitation and special newborn care sit in BEmOC or CEmOC in your textbook.",

"id": "np1-newborn-screening",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Newborn Screening",
"body": "## What it is\nNewborn screening (NBS) is a blood test done on the newborn to detect certain treatable inherited metabolic and endocrine disorders before symptoms appear, so treatment can prevent disability or death. In the Philippines it is backed by the Newborn Screening Act (commonly cited as RA 9288; verify). It is one of the child-health programs under the family health services.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- A few drops of blood from a heel prick are collected on special filter paper and dried.\n- Timing: the maternal health handout says between 48 hours and 2 weeks after birth; other teaching says after 24 hours of life, ideally 48 to 72 hours, once the baby has fed for some time. Testing too early can miss disorders. Special rules apply to babies who are preterm or sick.\n- Core disorders usually listed: congenital hypothyroidism, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, galactosemia, phenylketonuria, and G6PD deficiency (maple syrup urine disease is often added). The program has expanded the panel; verify the current list.\n- What each does if missed: congenital hypothyroidism (low thyroid hormone) causes intellectual disability and poor growth; phenylketonuria (cannot process phenylalanine) causes brain damage; galactosemia (cannot process milk sugar) causes liver damage and sepsis risk; congenital adrenal hyperplasia causes salt loss and life-threatening crisis; G6PD deficiency causes hemolysis when triggered by certain foods (fava beans), mothballs and drugs.\n- Screening is NOT a diagnosis. A positive screen needs a confirmatory test and prompt treatment.\n- Untreated, these disorders may cause intellectual disability, growth problems or death.\n- Parents have a right to be informed; refusal requires documentation per policy.\n- Newborn hearing screening is a separate program (commonly RA 9709); verify.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Explain the test to parents and get consent/documentation.\n2. Collect the sample at the correct time using the correct site (the side of the heel), after warming the foot.\n3. Fill in the card completely and let the blood dry on a flat surface, away from heat, sunlight and contact.\n4. Send the sample to the screening center promptly (do not stack wet cards).\n5. Trace babies with positive or unclear results quickly, because early treatment matters.\n6. Teach parents to follow up for results.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Why the heel: it is easy to reach with minimal harm if the correct lateral area is used.\n- Do not squeeze the heel repeatedly; do not layer blood or smear; fill each circle completely from one side; do not touch the paper.\n- Birth registration and the newborn screening card are both taught at postpartum visits.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nExplain that a normal-looking baby can still have a disorder, that the test is important even if there is no family history, and that parents should ask for the result. If the result is positive, bring the baby back at once for confirmation and start treatment on schedule.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Timing, specimen handling errors and the meaning of a positive screen.\n- Trap: a positive screen is not a diagnosis. Collecting too early can miss disorders, and collecting too late delays treatment, so use the correct window.\n- Trap: a filled-in but wet, smeared or contaminated card is rejected and the test is repeated.\n- Which disorder matches which consequence (hypothyroidism = intellectual disability).\n\n## Related topics\n[[EINC and Active Management of Third Stage of Labor]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Maternal Health Program: Prenatal Visits, Supplements and Postpartum Care]], [[Newborn Vital Signs and Heat Conservation]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The exact window (the handout says 48 hours to 2 weeks), the number of disorders in the current panel and what happens to babies born at home (verify local policy).\n- The cause-and-effect list above is general medical teaching, not taken from the handout.\n- Hearing screening law number and its inclusion in this note's scope.",

"id": "np1-vawc",

"title": "VAWC and Violence Against Women and Children",
"body": "## What it is\nVAWC means Violence Against Women and their Children. The Philippine law is commonly cited as RA 9262, the Anti-Violence Against Women and Their Children Act of 2004 (verify). It covers abuse by a husband, former partner or someone with whom the woman has or had a sexual or dating relationship, or with whom she has a common child. Violence against women and children is also one of the elements of the Reproductive Health program.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Forms of abuse: physical, sexual, psychological/emotional, and economic (controlling money, withholding support).\n- Protection orders can be sought: a barangay protection order (BPO) from the barangay, and temporary and permanent protection orders from the court. Violation can be punished. A BPO is usually issued quickly and is short-lived; the court orders last longer (verify the days).\n- Health workers have roles in screening, treatment, documentation and referral. Hospitals have a Women and Children Protection Unit (WCPU), and barangays are required to have a VAW desk.\n- Abuse can affect children directly or through witnessing. A separate child-protection law exists (commonly cited as RA 7610; verify). Rape is covered by a separate anti-rape law, and survivor care is covered in the psychiatric notes.\n- Warning signs: unexplained or repeated injuries, injuries in different stages of healing, fearful behavior, delay in seeking care, inconsistent story, injuries in places usually covered, vague complaints, a partner who will not leave her side.\n- The cycle of violence is often described: tension building, acute violence, then a reconciliation or \"honeymoon\" phase, and then it repeats.\n- Battered woman syndrome is recognized in the law as a defense in some cases.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Treat injuries and ensure immediate safety first. Life and safety come before the legal process (physiologic and safety needs before the rest).\n2. Provide a private, calm, nonjudgmental setting. Believe the client; do not blame. Interview her alone, away from the partner.\n3. Document objectively using the client's own words and body maps; photograph injuries if she agrees.\n4. Preserve evidence if the client agrees and per protocol.\n5. Refer to the Women and Children Protection Unit/desk, social worker, police and legal help.\n6. Safety plan: where to go, who to call, important documents, an emergency bag, a code word with a trusted person.\n7. Respect confidentiality and the client's pace of decision.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nAbuse is not the victim's fault, help is available, keep emergency numbers and a safety plan, and know where the nearest protection office is.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- First action with a suspected abuse victim: ensure safety and treat injuries; not confronting the abuser.\n- Trap: do not pressure the client to leave or press charges immediately.\n- Trap: documenting objectively means quoting the client, not writing \"alleged\" or your own conclusion.\n- Which person issues the BPO (the barangay official) versus the court orders.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Reproductive Health and Family Planning]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Community Mental Health Program]], [[Rape, Rape Trauma Syndrome and Survivor Care]], [[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact law provisions, the sequence and duration of protection orders and mandatory reporting rules for health workers (verify local policy).\n- The handout set had no VAWC section; the barangay and court order details, WCPU, VAW desk and battered woman syndrome come from general Philippine nursing teaching.\n- Law numbers (RA 9262, RA 7610) are the commonly cited ones; verify.",

"id": "np1-essential-drugs-herbal",

"title": "Essential Drugs",
"body": "## What it is\nEssential drugs are medicines that satisfy the priority health needs of most people and should be available at a price the community can afford. Philippine PHC also promotes generic medicines and tested herbal medicines as lower-cost options. In far-flung barangays, the Botika ng Barangay brings these medicines within reach.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Generic medicines use the chemical (generic) name and are cheaper than brands with the same active ingredient. A Generics Act exists (commonly cited as RA 6675; verify). The Philippine National Drug Formulary lists essential medicines.\n- The Traditional and Alternative Medicine Act (commonly cited as RA 8423; verify) supports research and use of traditional medicine.\n- General guidelines for medicinal plants (handout): use the right plant for the purpose; use only the part recommended; follow the amount and directions; use only one kind of plant at a time; stop if there is any reaction; if there is no improvement after two or three doses, consult a physician; boil in enamel or clay pots, not aluminum, and clean the pot before and after; avoid plants sprayed with insecticides.\n- DOH-endorsed herbal plants with their main use, scientific name and one preparation fact (handout; verify each with your textbook):\n  - Lagundi (Vitex negundo): cough and asthma; boil leaves in water for about 15 minutes; divide into three parts and drink morning, noon and night.\n  - Yerba buena (Mentha cordifolia): body pain; leaves boiled uncovered about 15 minutes; fresh leaves can be squeezed onto the painful part.\n  - Sambong (Blumea balsamifera): swelling, diuretic, helps prevent kidney stones; boiled uncovered about 15 minutes.\n  - Tsaang gubat (Ehretia microphylla): stomachache; boiled uncovered about 15 minutes; taken in two parts, one every four hours.\n  - Niyog-niyogan (Quisqualis indica): roundworm (ascaris); mature dried seeds eaten two hours after supper in an age-based number; do not eat extra seeds.\n  - Bayabas (Psidium guajava): cleansing wounds, mouth infections and swollen gums; wash wounds twice a day; use lukewarm solution as a mouthwash.\n  - Akapulko (Cassia alata): infected skin, skin irritation, scabies and fungal skin infection; apply the juice of squeezed leaves twice a day.\n  - Ulasimang bato (pansit-pansitan, Peperomia pellucida): lowers uric acid, for arthritis or gout; eaten as salad or boiled as decoction three times a day.\n  - Bawang (garlic, Allium sativum): lowers cholesterol; two cloves with meals three times a day (handout).\n  - Ampalaya (Momordica charantia): lowers blood sugar in mild diabetes; leaf decoction taken before meals.\n- Herbal medicines complement but do not replace proven treatment for serious illness.\n- Botika ng Barangay (handout): a community-run drug outlet in a far-flung, depressed barangay, run by a recognized community group or cooperative, with a licensed pharmacist who supervises, at least two trained barangay health workers as aides, and start-up funds shared by the community and local government (at least one third each).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the client's condition, allergies, current drugs and use of herbs. Safety checks come first.\n2. Refer serious or unresponsive illness; do not delay needed treatment for herbal remedies.\n3. Teach correct preparation, dose and duration of the herbal remedy using verified sources.\n4. Teach that herbs can interact with prescribed drugs.\n5. Promote generic names and rational drug use (right drug, right dose, right time, completing courses).\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Rational drug use: using medicine only when needed, correctly.\n- Decoction: boiling plant parts; the standard preparation for many leaves.\n- Barangay health worker: trained volunteer who may keep small stocks of essential medicines under supervision.\n\n## Memory aid\nPair each plant with one organ system or symptom (cough, kidney, skin, worms, sugar, uric acid). That pairing method is the aid; the pairs above are standard teaching.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Match the herb to its use, usually ampalaya, lagundi, akapulko, sambong, niyog-niyogan.\n- Trap: herb safety (some parts, doses or preparations are harmful) and not substituting for treatment.\n- Trap: aluminum pots and sprayed plants are wrong; one plant at a time.\n- Trap: \"no improvement after two or three doses\" means consult a doctor, not increase the dose.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions|DOH]], [[Herbal Medicine: General Guidelines]], [[Common Philippine Herbal Plants and Their Uses]], [[Botika ng Barangay]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The official list of herbs, plant parts used, preparation methods and amounts (verify local policy). The preparation amounts in the handout are age-based tables; confirm them before teaching them.\n- Uses differ slightly between sources: akapulko is taught as antifungal (tinea) and, in the handout, for infected skin and scabies; tsaang gubat is taught for stomachache and diarrhea; the handout describes ampalaya for \"mild insulin-dependent\" diabetes, a wording that likely means mild type 2 (verify).\n- Whether the DOH list now has more than ten endorsed plants (verify).",

"id": "np1-nutrition",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Nutrition",
"body": "## What it is\nNutrition is a PHC element because malnutrition (too little, too much, or the wrong nutrients) raises the risk of infection, poor growth and death in children. The national nutrition program aims to improve quality of life through better nutrition, health and productivity. Programs combine growth monitoring, supplementation, food fortification and feeding support.\n\n## Must-know facts\nMalnutrition recognition:\n- Protein-energy malnutrition shows in the child's weight, height, and arm measurements compared with standards. Mid-upper arm circumference (MUAC) is a quick screening tool. Terms: underweight (low weight for age), stunting (low height for age, long-term), wasting (low weight for height, recent severe loss).\n- Marasmus: severe lack of overall energy intake. Very thin, wasted, \"old man\" face, hungry, no edema.\n- Kwashiorkor: lack of protein with relatively more starch. Edema (often feet and face), swollen belly, thin or discolored hair, skin changes, apathy and irritability.\n- Bilateral pitting edema in a child signals severe acute malnutrition, which is an urgent referral.\n- Overnutrition and obesity also count as malnutrition.\n\nMicronutrients: the three common deficiencies in the handout are vitamin A, iron and iodine.\n- Vitamin A: needed for vision and immunity. Deficiency causes night blindness, then dryness of the conjunctiva and cornea (Bitot's spots, xerophthalmia) and, in severe cases, corneal damage and blindness. Children are given supplementation at set ages through a program; verify schedule and doses.\n- Iron: deficiency causes anemia (pallor, fatigue); pregnant women and young children are at risk. Iron-folic acid tablets are given in pregnancy.\n- Iodine: deficiency causes goiter and developmental problems; iodized salt is a main protection.\n- Zinc is used with ORS for diarrhea in many programs (verify local policy).\n\nFood fortification:\n- Fortification means adding nutrients to commonly eaten foods. The Food Fortification Law (RA 8976) requires fortification of staples: rice, flour, edible oil and sugar (mandatory), while fortification of processed foods is voluntary. A separate salt iodization law exists. Fortified products may carry a program seal (\"Sangkap Pinoy\"). Verify the exact list and law numbers.\n\nProgram components in the handout: micronutrient supplementation, food fortification, essential maternal and child health service package, nutrition information, communication and education, home, school and community food production, food assistance, and livelihood assistance.\n\nInfant and young child feeding messages: start breastfeeding within one hour; exclusive breastfeeding for six months (no water or other foods); add suitable complementary foods at six months (timely, adequate, safe and properly fed); continue breastfeeding to two years and beyond. The handout lists AIDS as a reason not to breastfeed; current guidance may differ.\n\nRelated laws: the Milk Code (EO 51) controlling breast milk substitutes; the Rooming-In and Breastfeeding Act (RA 7600); the Expanded Breastfeeding Promotion Act (RA 10028).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Check for danger signs in the malnourished child first (poor appetite, dehydration, serious infection, severe edema): refer if present.\n2. Measure weight, length/height and MUAC; plot and classify.\n3. Give supplements per program schedule; feed and teach per classification.\n4. Counsel on breastfeeding, complementary foods and food groups using locally affordable foods.\n5. Follow up growth and household food security.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nStart complementary foods at the recommended age, give varied and fortified foods, use iodized salt, keep food clean, and bring the child for regular weighing.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Differentiate marasmus vs kwashiorkor from the described child.\n- Which micronutrient problem matches the sign described (night blindness = vitamin A; goiter = iodine; pallor = iron).\n- Which staples must be fortified by law (rice, flour, edible oil, sugar).\n- Trap: both conditions need nutrition rehabilitation under supervision; rapid refeeding is risky.\n- Trap: exclusive breastfeeding means water is also withheld.\n\n## Memory aid\nKwashiorkor = edema (\"swollen\"); marasmus = \"wasted\". My own simplification.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Community Mental Health Program]], [[Infant and Young Child Feeding]], [[Laws Protecting Infant Feeding and Food Fortification]], [[Maternal Health Program: Prenatal Visits, Supplements and Postpartum Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Vitamin A, iron and zinc schedules and doses (none stated here), exact fortification list, law numbers and program names (verify local policy).\n- The handout statement not to breastfeed in AIDS is older guidance; verify the current national policy.\n- Definitions of stunting, wasting and underweight cut-offs (standard deviation scores) were not in the handout; verify.",

"title": "Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services",
"body": "## What it is\nThis PHC element covers basic care of common communicable diseases (CD), noncommunicable diseases (NCD), mental health and injuries/emergencies at the community level, with referral when needed. The handout expands \"T\" as medical and emergency care, noncommunicable diseases and mental health.\n\n## Must-know facts\nClinic visit standard procedures at the health center (handout order): registration (greet, family record, chief complaint, history, physical exam); waiting time (priority numbers, first come first served except emergencies); triaging (program-based cases managed by protocol, non-program cases referred to the physician, emergency cases given first aid); clinical evaluation (the nurse reaches an evidence-based diagnosis and rational treatment under DOH programs and explains the illness); laboratory exams (use a designated referral laboratory); referral (two-way system, accompany the patient in an emergency); prescription and dispensing with proper instruction; health education with one-on-one counseling.\n\nCommunicable disease treatment:\n- Follow national protocols (for example for diarrhea, pneumonia, tuberculosis, malaria). Common principles: treat dehydration with oral rehydration, give antibiotics only when indicated, complete the course, and teach prevention.\n- Diarrhea: assess hydration, give ORS and continue feeding; refer for severe dehydration or blood in stool. IMCI hydration plans: A (home: more fluids and continue feeding), B (some dehydration: ORS under supervision), C (severe: urgent IV or referral).\n\nNoncommunicable diseases:\n- Major NCDs: cardiovascular disease (including hypertension), diabetes, cancer and chronic lung disease.\n- Shared risk factors: tobacco, harmful alcohol use, unhealthy diet, physical inactivity. These are modifiable; age, sex and family history are not.\n- Community roles: screening (blood pressure, blood sugar, risk assessment), lifestyle counseling, regular medication, early referral and follow-up. A package of essential NCD interventions for primary care exists (commonly PhilPEN); the local name and content: verify.\n- Cancer warning signs in secondary prevention teaching (CAUTION US) and breast self-examination.\n- Warning signs needing urgent care: severe chest pain, sudden weakness or speech difficulty, very high blood pressure with symptoms, very high or very low blood sugar with confusion.\n- Lifestyle-related disease prevention and control is one of the five core public health programs in the Sentrong Sigla quality standards.\n\nEmergency services in the community:\n- First aid and basic life support, bleeding control (direct pressure), splinting, wound care, burn cooling with clean running water, choking relief, and safe transport.\n- A functioning referral system: call ahead, send a referral note, transfer in a stable state with an escort when possible.\n- Emergency hotline and ambulance availability vary: verify local numbers and policy.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Primary survey: airway, breathing, circulation, then disability/exposure. Life threats first.\n2. Control bleeding and protect the neck when injury suspected.\n3. Call for help or arrange transport early; do not wait for the full assessment to refer an unstable client.\n4. Give first aid within scope and standing orders.\n5. Document and communicate to the receiving facility.\n6. Teach family prevention and follow-up.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Triage: sorting by urgency so the sickest get care first.\n- Referral: sending to a higher level; two-way (also referring back for follow-up).\n- Program-based case: a client whose problem has a DOH protocol (TB, pneumonia, diarrhea, malaria).\n\n## Community and family teaching\nKnow danger signs, keep emergency contacts visible, take medicine as prescribed, do not use unproven remedies in emergencies, learn basic first aid and CPR.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Priority questions: ABC beats everything; unstable before stable.\n- Which NCD risk factor is modifiable? Smoking, diet, activity.\n- Clinic visit: which step comes after triage? Clinical evaluation. What is done for emergency cases at triage? First aid.\n- Trap: choosing a long assessment or teaching before addressing an obvious emergency.\n- Trap: in the waiting line, emergency cases skip the queue.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Essential Drugs]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Nutrition]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Clinic Visit Standard Procedures]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]], [[Primary vs Secondary Survey]], [[Triage Basics and Three-Level Triage]], [[Sentrong Sigla Program]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Exact treatment protocols, program names (NCD packages), hotline numbers and referral levels (verify local policy). No drug doses are given here.\n- The three-plan hydration scheme is from IMCI teaching, not the handout; verify the current chart.\n- Whether your textbook lists the CAUTION US signs here or under cancer nursing.",

"id": "np1-water-toilet",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Water Supply and Toilet Facilities",
"body": "## What it is\nSafe water and proper excreta disposal are the two strongest tools against diarrheal diseases, typhoid, hepatitis A, cholera and worm infections in the community. The Environmental and Occupational Health Office of the DOH sets the approved facility types, and the Sanitation Code (PD 856) is the governing law.\n\n## Must-know facts\nWater supply facilities (the classic three-level classification used in Philippine sanitation teaching):\n- Level I (point source): a protected well or developed spring with an outlet but no distribution system. Serves about 15 to 25 households; the farthest user is not more than 250 meters away; yield roughly 40 to 140 liters per minute. Suited to rural areas with scattered houses.\n- Level II (communal faucet or standpost): a source or reservoir, a piped network and communal faucets. Farthest house not more than 25 meters from the faucet; designed to deliver about 40 to 80 liters per person per day; about 100 households; one faucet per 4 to 6 households. Suited to clustered houses.\n- Level III (waterworks system): piped connection to individual households (the handout cites the big city water utilities as examples).\n- Unapproved sources: open dug wells, unimproved springs and wells that need priming.\n- Disinfect a water source after it is newly built or repaired or improved. A waterworks system must supply safe, potable water in adequate quantity; new well sites need approval and compliance with sanitary requirements.\n- Typical safe-source rules: a well should be protected from contamination, sited at a safe distance from toilets and animal pens (textbooks give a distance, commonly 25 meters; verify), with a sealed top and drainage away.\n- Home water treatment: boiling at a rolling boil (the handout says at least 2 minutes), chlorination, filtration and storing in a clean covered container. Verify local advice for chlorine amounts.\n- Water must be tested for bacteria if the source is suspect.\n\nToilet facilities (handout classification):\n- Level I: non-water-carriage toilets (pit latrines, reed odorless earth closet) and toilets needing little water (pour-flush toilets, aqua privies).\n- Level II: water-carriage toilets with a septic vault or tank for disposal.\n- Level III: water-carriage toilets connected to septic tanks and/or a sewerage system leading to a treatment plant.\n- A sanitary toilet prevents contact with excreta, keeps flies and animals away, prevents odor and does not contaminate water.\n- Placement: away from the water source and downhill from wells where possible.\n- Open defecation is unsafe.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the source of drinking water, how it is stored and what toilets the household uses.\n2. Identify the contamination risk (shared drain, nearby toilet, open container).\n3. Advise the quickest safe fix: boil or chlorinate drinking water now (an immediate barrier before construction).\n4. Promote construction and use of a sanitary toilet with the barangay and household.\n5. Teach handwashing with soap after toilet use and before eating or feeding.\n6. Report suspected waterborne disease clusters.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Potable: safe to drink.\n- Rolling boil: continuous bubbling; time per handout is at least 2 minutes.\n- Chlorination requires the right amount and contact time; do not state numbers from memory.\n- Excreta-borne routes: fecal-oral spread through water, hands, flies and food.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Classify a described facility as Level I, II or III. Key words: well or spring = I; communal tap = II; house connection = III.\n- Choose the best toilet for a family or the best first action for unsafe water.\n- Facts with numbers: 250 meters and 15 to 25 households (Level I); 25 meters and one faucet per 4 to 6 households (Level II).\n- Trap: a flushing toilet near a shallow well can still contaminate the well.\n- Trap: the toilet \"levels\" are a separate scale from the water levels, even though both use I, II, III.\n\n## Memory aid\nLevel number = how close the water is to the house: I at a source, II at a shared tap, III inside the home. My own aid.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Nutrition]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Typhoid Fever]], [[Cholera]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Household counts per level, safe distances, boiling times and chlorine amounts (verify local policy).\n- The handout gives 2 minutes of rolling boil in its food-safety section; other books say 1 to 5 minutes or longer at altitude (verify).\n- The toilet-level grouping (which types are Level I versus II) differs in some books.",

"id": "np1-waste-food-laws",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws",
"body": "## What it is\nThis note groups three parts of environmental health: how waste is separated and disposed of, how food is kept safe, and the main laws that govern sanitation.\n\n## Must-know facts\nWaste:\n- Segregate at source: biodegradable (food scraps, leaves), non-biodegradable/residual, recyclable, and special or hazardous waste (batteries, chemicals).\n- Health-care waste is separated into infectious/pathological, sharps, chemical, pharmaceutical, radioactive and general waste. Sharps go into puncture-resistant containers; infectious waste goes into designated bags and containers.\n- Color coding is used for bins and bags. The fundamentals handout lists: black = non-biodegradable, green = biodegradable, yellow = infectious, red = puncture-resistant sharps, orange = radioactive. The assignment of colors is regulated and has changed over time, so verify the current DOH health-care waste and local solid-waste color codes.\n- Safe disposal methods include composting, sanitary landfill, controlled treatment (such as autoclaving) and the barangay materials recovery facility. Open burning and open dumping are discouraged.\n\nFood sanitation:\n- Food-borne illness comes from contaminated food, hands, water, pests and poor storage.\n- The four \"rights\" of food safety (handout):\n  - Right source: buy fresh food; check expiry dates; avoid dented, bulging or broken-seal cans; boil water (rolling boil at least 2 minutes).\n  - Right preparation: keep raw and cooked foods apart; use pasteurized milk and juice; wash vegetables eaten raw; wash hands and utensils before and after preparing food; keep the kitchen floor clean.\n  - Right cooking: cook thoroughly so all parts reach about 70 degrees Celsius; eat promptly; wash hands before and after eating.\n  - Right storage: cooked food not left at room temperature more than two hours; use tightly sealed containers; if keeping longer than about 4 to 5 hours, keep hot (at or above 60 C) or cold (10 C or below per the handout); do not overload the refrigerator with warm food; reheat to at least 70 C. \"When in doubt, throw it out.\"\n- Food handlers need regular health examinations/certificates and should not handle food when ill with diarrhea, skin infection or respiratory infection. The DOH requires laboratories to examine food handlers' stools by the formalin-ether concentration technique, not the direct smear. Establishments need sanitary permits and are inspected for source, containers, transport and handler certificates.\n- Food establishments are rated Class A (excellent), Class B (very satisfactory) and Class C (satisfactory).\n- Control flies, cockroaches and rodents with clean storage and waste management.\n\nLaws (commonly cited in nursing reviews; verify numbers and content):\n- Code on Sanitation of the Philippines (PD 856): water, food, excreta, waste, housing and other sanitation rules.\n- Toxic Substances and Hazardous and Nuclear Waste Control Act (RA 6969, 1990).\n- Ecological Solid Waste Management Act (RA 9003, 2000): segregation and local solid waste plans.\n- Clean Water Act (RA 9275, 2004) and Clean Air Act (RA 8749, 1999): water and air quality.\n- Mandatory notifiable disease reporting law (commonly RA 11332).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Inspect and assess hazards (open dump, fly breeding, food handling, unsafe storage).\n2. Protect health first: isolate or treat sick food handlers or affected persons, then remove the hazard.\n3. Teach and demonstrate segregation, handwashing and safe food preparation.\n4. Work with the barangay sanitary inspector and officials for enforcement.\n5. Report outbreaks and keep records.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Sanitary inspector: the local officer who enforces sanitation rules.\n- Vector: insects and rodents that carry disease; remove food, water and shelter for them.\n- Health certificate: required for food handlers, cooks and helpers, renewed on schedule.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Which bin or container is right for a given waste? Reason through category first, then check your color table.\n- Which action is the first step to prevent food poisoning in a community? Prevention via handwashing, cooking and storage beats treatment.\n- Link a law to its topic (food, solid waste, water, air, toxic waste).\n- Trap: food safety numbers (70 C core, 2 hours at room temperature, 60 C hot and 10 C or lower cold per the handout).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Water Supply and Toilet Facilities]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions|DOH]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Typhoid Fever]], [[Isolation Categories and Precautions]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- The color code tables (the handout list is a nursing-fundamentals version, not necessarily the DOH regulation), the law numbers, the food holding temperatures and the health certificate rules (verify local policy).\n- The handout's cold-holding limit of 10 C is higher than the 5 C commonly taught elsewhere; verify.\n- The rating classes for food establishments and which office issues the sanitary permit.",

"id": "np1-mental-health-program",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community Mental Health Program",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Mental Health Program brings basic mental health care into primary care and the community, so people can get help close to home, without stigma. The national law is commonly cited as the Mental Health Act (RA 11036, 2018; verify). Mental health is listed with medical and emergency care and noncommunicable diseases under the \"T\" element of PHC.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Mental health is a part of overall health. Community care tries to prevent problems, find them early, treat and support recovery in the community instead of institutions.\n- Services are integrated into primary care, with referral to specialists and hospitals for severe or complex cases.\n- Rights approach: dignity, informed consent, privacy and protection against discrimination.\n- Common community concerns: depression, anxiety, substance use, suicide risk, stress after disasters and violence, dementia in older adults, and mental health of adolescents.\n- Warning signs of risk: talking about death or wanting to die, giving away belongings, hopelessness, withdrawal, sudden changes in behavior, substance misuse, major losses. Higher risk when there is a specific plan, available means and a past attempt.\n- Stigma and myths prevent care-seeking; education reduces them.\n- Barangay and community volunteers can be trained to recognize problems and refer.\n- Levels of prevention in mental health (handout lists mental health rehabilitation as an example of tertiary prevention): primary = promotion, coping skills and stigma reduction; secondary = early detection and treatment; tertiary = rehabilitation and relapse prevention.\n- Psychological first aid after crises and disasters is commonly taught as look, listen, link: check safety and needs, listen without forcing the person to talk, and connect to information and services.\n- A crisis is a temporary state of disorganization when usual coping fails; crisis intervention is short-term, focused on safety and restoring function.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Ensure safety first: if a person is at risk of harming self or others, stay with them, remove means, and get urgent help. Safety is the first priority (Maslow safety need).\n2. Build rapport and listen without judgment; ask directly about suicidal thoughts when concerned, since asking does not plant the idea.\n3. Assess needs and basic functioning (sleep, eating, support).\n4. Provide psychological first aid and stress reduction when a crisis or disaster has happened.\n5. Refer for evaluation and treatment, arrange follow-up and involve family.\n6. Teach coping skills, healthy routines and how to get help.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Psychological first aid: practical, supportive care that offers comfort, safety and information without forcing the person to talk.\n- Primary, secondary and tertiary prevention applies: promotion and stigma reduction, early detection, then rehabilitation and relapse prevention.\n- Therapeutic responses: open-ended, reflective, empathic; avoid advice-giving, false reassurance and \"why\" questions.\n\n## Community and family teaching\nMental illness is treatable, it is not shameful or caused by weakness, encourage taking medicines as prescribed, keep a routine, avoid alcohol and drugs, and know crisis contacts (verify local numbers).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\n- Safety first with a person expressing suicidal thoughts, then therapeutic communication, then referral.\n- Therapeutic responses: open, empathic, not false reassurance.\n- Trap: arguing with or minimizing feelings; promising to keep a suicide plan secret.\n- Trap: leaving a suicidal person alone to \"give them privacy.\"\n- Which level of prevention: rehabilitation of a recovering client is tertiary.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Basics|Primary Health Care]], [[VAWC and Violence Against Women and Children]], [[Health Promotion: Concepts, Diet and Exercise|Health Promotion]], [[Nutrition]], [[Crisis and Crisis Intervention]], [[Therapeutic Communication]], [[Major Depressive Disorder]], [[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts]], [[Three Levels of Prevention]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Program names, the current guideline for community mental health in primary care, and local hotline numbers (verify local policy).\n- The handout gave almost no detail on this program; the psychological first aid steps and suicide risk factors come from general psychiatric nursing teaching.\n- The Mental Health Act's number and year, and which services it assigns to the barangay and the LGU.",

"title": "Epidemiology and Endemic Diseases",
"body": "## What it is\nThe \"Locally endemic diseases\" element of PHC: understanding disease patterns in the community with data, and controlling the diseases that are regularly present. This is a hub note that points to the detailed notes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Epidemiology and surveillance data guide which diseases to control: see [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]].\n- The diseases that are regularly present in an area, with cause, spread, signs and control: see [[Locally Endemic Diseases]].\n- Numbers used to describe community health: [[Vital Statistics]] and [[Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid]].\n- Patterns of occurrence: sporadic (few scattered cases), endemic (usual constant level), epidemic (unusually many in a short time), pandemic (several countries). Handout examples of endemic areas: schistosomiasis in Leyte and Samar, filariasis in Sorsogon, malaria in Palawan.\n- Control follows the epidemiologic triangle (agent, host, environment) and the chain of infection; the easiest link to break is the mode of transmission.\n- Disease detail notes for the commonly endemic infections: [[Tuberculosis: Features and Diagnosis]], [[Dengue Fever]], [[Malaria]], [[Schistosomiasis]], [[Filariasis]], [[Leprosy (Hansen's Disease)]], [[Rabies]], [[Leptospirosis]].\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Treat urgent cases first, then report and investigate, because early reporting stops spread.\n2. Analyze local data and target the biggest or fastest-growing problem.\n3. Break the chain of transmission at the weakest link (agent, host or environment).\n4. Use the levels of prevention: protect the well, find cases early, rehabilitate the affected.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nIdentify the disease from vector or reservoir, choose the control that breaks transmission, or compute a rate. Trap: epidemic is judged against the expected level of that place, not by raw numbers.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Locally Endemic Diseases]], [[Vital Statistics]], [[Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid]], [[Three Levels of Prevention]], [[Elements of Primary Health Care]], [[Chain of Infection and Modes of Transmission]], [[Epidemiologic Triangle and Patterns of Disease Occurrence]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\n- Which diseases your textbook treats as locally endemic (verify local policy).\n- Endemic areas change as control programs succeed; the provinces named here come from one handout.",

Environmental health is the branch of public health that prevents illness by managing the environment and changing behavior to cut exposure to harmful agents. This is a hub note that points to the detailed notes.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Water supply levels and toilet types: [[Water Supply and Toilet Facilities]].\n- Waste segregation, food safety and the main sanitation laws: [[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]].\n- Poor sanitation drives diarrheal disease, typhoid, hepatitis A, worm infections and vector-borne disease, so sanitation is prevention at the primary level.\n- Core laws to recognize: PD 856 (Sanitation Code), RA 6969 (toxic and hazardous waste), RA 8749 (Clean Air), RA 9003 (solid waste), RA 9275 (Clean Water).\n- Core numbers to recognize: Level I water serves about 15 to 25 households; Level II has a faucet per 4 to 6 households within 25 meters; food cooked to about 70 C; cooked food not left out more than 2 hours.\n- Environmental sanitation also covers housing, vector control, and the Sentrong Sigla core program on environmental health.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. "area": "NP1",
"title": "Public Health Nurse Roles and Qualifications",
"body": "## What it is\nThe public health nurse (PHN) is a nurse working for a local or national health office or a public school, whatever the job title. The role covers many hats at once, and the exam likes to ask which hat a described action belongs to.\n\n## Roles and what each looks like\n- Planner/programmer: identifies health needs, priorities and problems of individuals, families and the community.\n- Nursing care provider: cares for the sick and disabled at home, clinic, school or workplace.\n- Manager/supervisor: writes and carries out the nursing plan and leads people to solve their own health problems.\n- Community organizer: motivates participation and starts or joins community development work (see [[Community Organizing (COPAR) Basic Principles]]).\n- Service coordinator: works with individuals, families and groups to link them to services.\n- Health educator/counselor/trainer: teaches, counsels, trains rural health midwives, speaks as a resource person.\n- Health monitor: tracks the status of clients through repeated contacts.\n- Role model: lives a healthy lifestyle visibly.\n- Change agent: motivates changes in health behavior.\n- Reporter/recorder/statistician: records every intervention, updates the database, analyzes data (links to [[Epidemiology and FHSIS]]).\n- Researcher: uses observation, interview, survey and physical exam for assessment.\n\n## Qualifications (as listed in the handout)\n- BSN graduate and registered nurse, in good physical and mental health.\n- Interest and willingness to work in the community; able to work with other disciplines and community members.\n- Able to analyze factors that affect population health, apply the nursing process and mobilize community resources.\n- Leadership potential, resourcefulness, active membership in professional nursing organizations.\n- Salary grade given in the handout: 15.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the action to the role: a nurse teaching midwives is trainer; a nurse who tallies cases is recorder/statistician; a nurse who motivates smoking cessation is change agent.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Community Health Nursing Basics]], [[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSalary grade and the exact qualification list can change with civil service rules.",

"title": "Health Sector Reform Agenda and FOURmula One",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Health Sector Reform Agenda (HSRA) is described as the overriding goal of the DOH. FOURmula One for Health (2005-2010) was the framework for putting HSRA into action.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Goals: better health outcomes, more responsive health systems, equitable health care financing.\n- Four elements of the strategy (match each to its purpose):\n  - Health financing: more, better and sustained investment in health (PhilHealth is the example).\n  - Health regulation: quality and affordability of health goods and services.\n  - Health service delivery: accessibility and availability of basic and essential care.\n  - Good governance: better health system performance at national and local levels.\n- DOH vision in the handout: Filipinos among the healthiest in Southeast Asia by 2022 and Asia by 2040. Mission: lead a productive, resilient, equitable, people-centered health system.\n- DOH roles: leader in health (policy, plans, advocacy), enabler and capacity builder (oversight, quality standards), administrator of specific services (selected national hospitals, emergency response).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nGiven a program (for example, PhilHealth expansion), name which of the four elements it belongs to. Financing is the PhilHealth answer.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Department of Health (DOH) Concepts and Functions]], [[Local Health System and Inter-Local Health Zones]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVision years and later frameworks (for example, universal health care) may have replaced these dates.",

"title": "Local Health System and Inter-Local Health Zones",
"body": "## What it is\nUnder RA 7160 (Local Government Code), health structures, staff and budgets from the provincial level down to barangays were devolved to local government units (LGUs). The inter-local health zone (ILHZ) is the DOH-promoted system similar to a district health system.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Objectives: set up a local health system, upgrade management and service capability of local facilities, promote inter-LGU links and cost sharing, involve the private sector, NGOs and community.\n- Four parts of a health zone:\n  - People: an ideal district serves about 100,000 to 500,000 for efficiency.\n  - Boundaries: clear lines decide who is accountable.\n  - Health facilities: district or provincial hospital and other services working as one integrated system.\n  - Health workers: the right mix of providers for comprehensive services.\n\n## Why it matters\nDevolution made LGUs responsible for primary care, which is why the PHN works under the local government but follows DOH program protocols.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Health Sector Reform Agenda and FOURmula One]], [[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]], [[Primary Health Care Basics]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPopulation size range is as given by the handout.",

"area": "NP1",
"title": "Levels of Health Care and the Referral System",
"body": "## What it is\nCare is organized into three levels, and clients are referred upward when needs exceed what a facility can provide. Referral should be two-way: clients also return to the lower level for follow-up.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Primary level: first contact, devolved to cities and municipalities. Staff: center physicians, public health nurses, rural health midwives, barangay health workers, traditional healers. Examples: barangay health station, rural health unit, health center, community hospital, puericulture center.\n- Secondary level: physicians with basic training; referral center for primary facilities; can do minor surgery and simple laboratory tests. Examples: district or emergency hospital, provincial or city hospital.\n- Tertiary level: specialists; handles complicated cases and intensive care; referral center for secondary facilities. Examples: regional and national medical centers, teaching hospitals.\n- Primary health care worker levels: village or barangay health worker (trained community health worker, health auxiliary volunteer, traditional birth attendant or healer) and intermediate level (public health nurse, midwife, sanitary inspector, rural health physician).\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nName the level of a given facility, or decide where to send a client: simple cases stay at primary; accompany the client during an emergency referral.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Primary Health Care Basics]], [[Primary Health Care Pillars and Characteristics]], [[Clinic Visit Standard Procedures]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWhether a community hospital counts as primary or secondary varies by source; the handout lists it under primary.",

"id": "np1-g-management-functions",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Management Functions of the Community Health Nurse",
"body": "## What it is\nThe CHN manages programs and people using six functions. Know them in order and what each means.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Planning: build a vision, decide what must be done and what the organization wants to achieve.\n- Organizing: decide how to carry out the plan; major concerns are analysis of systems, analysis of functions, assigning job responsibilities, and implementation.\n- Directing: tell workers what was decided in planning and organizing.\n- Coordinating: link members of the health team so objectives are met.\n- Controlling: measure and correct activities; steps are establishing standards, measuring performance against criteria, and correcting deviations.\n- Evaluating: judge the effectiveness of actions to decide future action, by documenting progress and comparing achievements with a performance standard.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Plan first, since every later function depends on a clear target.\n2. Organize people and resources.\n3. Direct and coordinate the team.\n4. Control and evaluate, then feed results into the next plan.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if staff do not know their tasks, directing or organizing failed; if results drift from the target, controlling needs to correct deviations; if you want to know whether a program worked, evaluate.\n- Order: plan, organize, direct, coordinate, control, evaluate. Controlling includes standards; evaluating includes the final judgment.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nScenarios: writing job descriptions is organizing; checking staff output against standards is controlling; holding a meeting to relay the plan is directing. Trap: confusing controlling (correcting during the work) with evaluating (judging results).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Planning and Prioritization]], [[Public Health Nurse Roles and Qualifications]], [[Management Basics, Roles and Skills]], [[Management Levels and the POLE Process]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSome books use four or five functions (POLC); match your reviewer.\n",

"title": "Clinic Visit Standard Procedures",
"body": "## What it is\nThe standard flow a client follows in a health center, with the nurse managing program-based cases by protocol.\n\n## Steps in order\n1. Registration or admission: greet and build rapport, prepare a family record (new client) or retrieve it (old client), record chief complaint and history, do physical exam.\n2. Waiting time: give priority numbers; first come first served, except emergencies.\n3. Triaging: manage program-based cases per protocol, refer non-program cases to the physician, give first aid to emergencies.\n4. Clinical evaluation: validate history and exam, reach an evidence-based diagnosis and treatment based on DOH programs, explain illness, treatment and prevention.\n5. Laboratory and diagnostics: identify a designated referral laboratory when needed.\n6. Referral: two-way referral; accompany the client in an emergency.\n7. Prescription or dispensing: teach correct drug intake.\n8. Health education: one-on-one counseling, reinforce messages, set the next appointment.\n\n## Priority reasoning\nEmergencies skip the queue (safety and ABC first). Everything else follows order of arrival.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Levels of Health Care and the Referral System]], [[Home Visit and Bag Technique]], [[Blood Pressure Measurement Technique]].",

"id": "np1-g-bp-technique",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Blood Pressure Measurement Technique",
"body": "## What it is\nCommunity-level BP measurement steps. Technique errors are a favorite exam target.\n\n## Preparation\n- Client relaxed and rested 5 minutes; no smoking or caffeine in the previous 30 minutes.\n- Explain the procedure; seated or supine position.\n\n## Applying equipment\n- Bare the arm; cuff 2-3 cm above the brachial artery, snug without creases.\n- Manometer at eye level; arm at heart level (supported on a table or chair arm; at the side if lying).\n- Palpate the brachial pulse just below or slightly medial to the antecubital area.\n\n## Palpatory then auscultatory\n- Palpate: inflate until the pulse disappears; note that value as palpated systolic; deflate fully and wait 1-2 minutes.\n- Auscultate: stethoscope over the brachial pulse; inflate to 30 mmHg above the palpated systolic; deflate slowly at 2-3 mmHg per beat.\n- First clear tapping sound (Korotkoff phase I) is systolic; disappearance of sound (phase V) is diastolic.\n\n## Recording\n- At each visit, take the mean of 2 readings at least 2 minutes apart.\n- First visit: measure both arms; later use the arm with the higher reading.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says \"use the bell side\" but then says the diaphragm for obese clients; most textbooks use the diaphragm for Korotkoff sounds and the bell for low sounds. Confirm which your school teaches.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Clinic Visit Standard Procedures]], [[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]].",

"id": "np1-g-home-visit-bag",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Home Visit and Bag Technique",
"body": "## What it is\nA home visit is a professional family-nurse contact for assessing home and family situations and giving care. The bag technique is the method that keeps the visit efficient and prevents spread of infection.\n\n## Home visit principles and purposes\n- Must have a purpose; plan using available information, involve the family, prioritize essential needs.\n- Purposes: give care, assess living conditions, teach prevention and control, link agencies and the public, use the referral system.\n- Frequency depends on (most important first): family needs, then family acceptance, then agency policy, other agencies involved, past services, ability to recognize own needs.\n\n## Steps\n1. Greet and introduce self. 2. State purpose. 3. Observe and determine needs. 4. Place the bag in a convenient place and do the bag technique. 5. Give care and teaching. 6. Record. 7. Set a return visit.\n\n## Bag technique\n- Public health bag: essential equipment carried on every visit.\n- Principles: minimize or prevent spread of infection; save time and effort; show total care effectiveness; may vary by agency or home as long as infection transfer is avoided.\n- Bag contents: all needed supplies and emergency items, cleaned often and replaced, protected from contact with other articles, arranged for the user's convenience.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation]], [[Clinic Visit Standard Procedures]].",

"id": "np1-g-fp-permanent-hormonal",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Family Planning Methods: Permanent, Hormonal and Barrier",
"body": "## Goal and role\nGoal: universal access to FP information and services; helps couples reach desired family size under responsible parenthood and lowers infant, neonatal, under-five and maternal deaths. PHN roles: counseling, providing service packages, ensuring supplies. Common misconceptions: causes abortion, makes couples sterile, kills sexual desire.\n\n## Methods\n- Female sterilization (bilateral tubal ligation): permanent; fallopian tubes cut or blocked. Pros: no effect on sex or breastfeeding. Cons: surgical risks (infection, bleeding), ectopic pregnancy risk, hard to reverse, no STI protection.\n- Male sterilization (vasectomy): vas deferens tied and cut or blocked via small scrotal opening. Effective after 3 months; mild pain and swelling 2-3 days; possible hematoma; reversal difficult and costly. Sexual ability and ejaculation are kept.\n- Pill: estrogen and progesterone. Reduces painful menses and endometriosis symptoms; must be taken consistently; may cause nausea, dizziness, breast tenderness and can suppress lactation.\n- Injectable: progestin; suppresses ovulation, thickens cervical mucus, alters uterine lining; reversible, no daily intake, no estrogen effects.\n- Male condom: latex sheath; no hormones and protects against STIs; latex allergy or reduced sensation possible.\n- LAM (lactational amenorrhea method): temporary postpartum method; works for a maximum of 6 months; effectiveness drops with separation or non-full breastfeeding.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nWhich method protects against STI (condom); which suppresses lactation (pill); LAM duration (6 months).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Fertility Awareness Family Planning Methods]], [[Reproductive Health and Family Planning]], [[Maternal and Child Health]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nCombined pills are generally avoided in breastfeeding; confirm eligibility rules.",

"id": "np1-g-fp-natural",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Fertility Awareness Family Planning Methods",
"body": "## What it is\nNatural methods identify fertile days so the couple abstains (or uses a barrier) on those days. They need cooperation of both partners.\n\n## Methods\n- Mucus/billings method: abstain on fertile days based on cervical mucus. Unsuitable with unusual vaginal discharge.\n- Basal body temperature: take temperature daily; a rise after ovulation marks the end of fertility. Handout figures: slight drop before ovulation and about 1 degree F rise at ovulation.\n- Sympto-thermal: combines mucus, temperature and other ovulation signs.\n- Two-day method: check for cervical secretions daily; any secretions today or yesterday mean fertile. Works for any cycle length, no cost, immediately reversible, involves the male partner; unreliable with abnormal secretions.\n- Standard days method: color-coded cycle beads mark fertile and infertile days; no side effects; can avoid or achieve pregnancy.\n\n## Cautions\n- Not for women whose pregnancy would be dangerous (reliability matters).\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout says the standard days method \"cannot be used by women who usually have cycles between 26 and 32 days\"; this is reversed. It is meant for women whose cycles are usually 26-32 days long and is unsuitable outside that range.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Planning Methods: Permanent, Hormonal and Barrier]], [[Reproductive Health and Family Planning]].",

"id": "np1-g-oral-health",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Oral Health Program",
"body": "## What it is\nA program to lower dental caries and periodontal disease and keep people orally fit.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Handout targets: caries prevalence from 92 percent (1998) to 85 percent, and periodontal disease from 78 percent to 60 percent, by 2010.\n- Objectives: raise the proportion of orally fit children under 6 to 80 percent by 2010; control oral health risk among young people; improve oral health of pregnant women by 20 percent and older persons by 10 percent every year until 2010.\n- Promotive: health education.\n- Preventive: oral examination, oral hygiene, pit and fissure sealant program, fluoride use.\n- Curative: permanent filling, gum treatment, atraumatic restorative treatment, temporary filling, extraction, post-extraction care, drainage of localized oral abscesses.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess teeth and gums.\n2. Teach brushing and diet.\n3. Refer for sealants, fillings or extraction.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: early decay without pain points to prevention (sealant, fluoride); an abscess needs curative drainage and referral.\n- Order: prevent first, treat what already exists.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSort activities into promotive, preventive or curative. Sealants and fluoride are preventive; extraction and fillings are curative.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Levels of Prevention]], [[Maternal and Child Health]], [[Child Health Programs Overview (Ages 0-9)]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTargets are dated (2010); verify current ones.\n",

"id": "np1-g-herbal-guidelines",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Herbal Medicine: General Guidelines",
"body": "## What it is\nRules for safe use of medicinal plants in community health teaching.\n\n## Must-know rules\n- Use the right plant for the purpose, the suggested plant part, and the recommended dose and directions.\n- Use one kind of plant at a time.\n- Stop if there is any untoward reaction or side effect.\n- If there is no improvement after two or three doses, consult a physician.\n- Boil in enamel or clay pots, not aluminum; clean the pot before and after boiling.\n- Avoid plants treated with insecticides.\n- Wash and chop plants before boiling.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Confirm the plant is the right one.\n2. Teach correct preparation and dose.\n3. Tell the client when to stop and when to see a doctor.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a client with a rash after a decoction should stop it, which is safety first; a client with no improvement should be referred.\n- Order: safety (stop on reaction), then effectiveness (dose and plant part), then referral. Herbs do not replace care for serious illness.\n- Avoid combining several herbs because a reaction could not be traced to one plant.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nSpot the unsafe instruction (aluminum pot, several plants at once). Trap: choosing to continue despite a reaction.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Common Philippine Herbal Plants and Their Uses]], [[Essential Drugs]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHerbal guidance is DOH-endorsed only for listed plants; verify the list.\n",

"id": "np1-g-herbal-plants",

"title": "Common Philippine Herbal Plants and Their Uses",
"body": "## What it is\nTen plants endorsed for community use. Memorize plant to use.\n\n## Plant, use and preparation notes\n- Lagundi (Vitex negundo): cough, asthma, fever. Boil chopped leaves in two cups of water 15 minutes on low fire; divide into three parts through the day (for fever, every four hours). Handout amounts: adult 6 tbsp fresh or 4 tbsp dried; ages 7-12 half of that; ages 2-6 about a quarter.\n- Yerba buena: body pain. Boil, leave uncovered, cool and strain; drink in three parts; fresh crushed leaves may be applied to painful areas.\n- Sambong (Blumea balsamifera): swelling, diuretic, helps urinary stones. Boil leaves uncovered.\n- Tsaang gubat: stomachache. Divide into two parts, one part every four hours.\n- Niyug-niyugan: ascariasis. Eat dried mature seeds two hours after supper; adult 8-10 seeds, fewer for children.\n- Bayabas (guava): wound cleansing, mouth infection, swollen gums. Wash wounds twice a day; lukewarm solution as mouthwash.\n- Akapulko (Cassia alata): infected skin, irritation, scabies. Apply squeezed leaf juice twice a day.\n- Ulasimang bato: lowers uric acid; for arthritis and gout. Eat leaves as salad or take decoction.\n- Bawang (garlic): lowers cholesterol. Two cloves with meals three times a day.\n- Ampalaya: mild diabetes. Boil leaves 15 minutes; drink half a glass three times a day before meals.\n\n## Check against your textbook\nDoses are from the handout and are unverified; the DOH list also names plants such as these with different forms (for example, tablets). Check the official list.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Herbal Medicine: General Guidelines]].",

"id": "np1-g-health-emergency-program",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Program",
"body": "## What it is\nThe program for preparing the public and strengthening the health sector to respond to emergencies and disasters. It stresses that emergencies need immediate response and are everyone's responsibility.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Goal: promote health emergency preparedness among the public and strengthen the health sector's capability to respond.\n- Legal basis: PD 1566 (1978), strengthening disaster control and community disaster preparedness; RA 7160, which transferred responsibilities to LGUs.\n- Disaster: serious disruption of society's functioning causing widespread human, material or environmental loss beyond what the society can cope with using its own resources. By cause: natural or man-made.\n- Emergency: requires immediate response, is everyone's responsibility, should be woven into community and administrative levels, and should focus on process and people rather than documentation.\n- Main objective: decrease mortality and morbidity and prevent disability.\n- Hazard: any phenomenon that could disrupt or damage people and their environment.\n- Principles: first priority is protecting people at risk; second is protecting critical resources and systems; disaster management is part of national development plans; relies on understanding hazard risk; capabilities must be built before impact; needs interdisciplinary collaboration.\n- Major risks: natural (flood, earthquake, cyclone), technological (chemical, radiological), epidemics, and societal (social exclusion, extreme poverty, group violence).\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Protect people at risk first.\n2. Protect critical resources and systems second.\n3. Prepare before the hazard: plan, train, stock.\n4. Respond, then help recovery.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: identify the hazard, find who is vulnerable, prepare capacity before impact.\n- Order: life before property; preparedness before response, because capabilities cannot be built during the event.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems ask the first priority (people) or the second (critical resources). Trap: choosing documentation over action.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Disaster Nursing Basic Concepts]], [[Disaster Management Continuum]], [[NDRRMC and the Philippine Disaster Framework]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nPD 1566 is old; current law is RA 10121 (see the NDRRMC note).\n",

"title": "National Voluntary Blood Services Program",
"body": "## What it is\nProgram under the Blood Services Act (RA 7719, 1994) promoting voluntary, non-profit blood donation and a safe, adequate supply.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Aims: encourage voluntary donation as a humanitarian act; provide safe, affordable, equitably distributed blood; involve all sectors.\n- Donor requirements: age 16-65 (parental consent at 16-17), good health, weight over 45 kg for 250 mL or 50 kg for 450 mL, BP 90-160 systolic and 60-100 diastolic, hemoglobin at least 12.5 g/dL.\n- Contraindications: diabetes, cancer, hyperthyroidism, cardiovascular, severe psychiatric, epilepsy, severe bronchitis, AIDS or syphilis and other STIs, malaria, kidney or liver disease, prolonged bleeding, prohibited drug use.\n- Storage: whole blood and red cells up to 5 weeks; plasma frozen up to 12 months.\n\n## Donor teaching after donation\n- Keep the dressing on 3 to 12 hours; bruising is normal; avoid heavy lifting with that arm.\n- No smoking for 2 hours, no alcohol for 12 hours; eat regular meals and drink more fluids.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Treatment of CD, NCD and Emergency Services]], [[Blood Pressure Measurement Technique]].\n\n## Check against your textbook\nEligibility values vary by blood center.",

"id": "np1-g-copar-activities-par",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "COPAR Ten Critical Activities and Participatory Action Research",
"body": "## What it is\nBeyond the phases, COPAR uses ten recurring activities and a research approach where the community does the investigating.\n\n## Ten critical activities\n- Integration: living with and sharing the people's experience to build trust.\n- Social investigation: systematic study of community structures and forces; gather geographic, economic, political and socio-cultural data, identify classes and sectors, decide approach.\n- Planning: choose specific activities.\n- Ground work (agitation): one-to-one motivation to act.\n- Community meeting: ratifies decisions and gives collective power.\n- Role play: rehearse the meeting with the target group.\n- Social mobilization: people's action to solve their problems.\n- Evaluation: what was done, left out, remains.\n- Reflection: analyze strengths and weaknesses of finished action.\n- Organization: widens participation and collective action.\n\n## Participatory action research\n- Beneficiaries are the main actors; blends education, research and action; purpose is empowerment.\n- Versus traditional research: problems are defined by community members, not outside experts; the community runs the investigation with external researchers working alongside, and formulates its own recommendations.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Community Organizing (COPAR) Phases]], [[Community Organizing (COPAR) Basic Principles]].",
