"id": "np1-x-chn-process",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community Health Nursing Process: Assessment to Evaluation",
"body": "## What it is\nThe community health nursing process applies assessment, planning, implementation and evaluation to a family, group or community. It is the same logic as the ordinary nursing process but the client is a population, so it asks how far a family or community is from the health it could achieve.\n\n## Must-know facts\nAssessment gives an estimate of the degree to which a family, group or community is reaching the level of health possible for it, identifies specific deficiencies or guidance needed, and estimates the possible effects of nursing interventions. Four kinds of findings are named in the handout:\n- Health deficit: a gap between actual and achievable health; failure in health maintenance; disease or disability already present, or developmental lag.\n- Health threat: a condition that promotes disease or injury and keeps people from reaching their health potential.\n- Foreseeable crisis: an anticipated period of unusual demand on the individual or family for resources and adjustment.\n- Wellness potential: a state of wellness and the likelihood that health will be maintained or improved, depending on the family's desire.\n\nPlanning has three parts: goal setting (the first step; a declaration of purpose giving direction), constructing a plan of action (choose courses of action, select types of nursing intervention, identify resources), and developing an operational plan (set priorities, phase and coordinate activities). Evaluation parameters are developed during planning.\n\nImplementation carries out the interventions set by the goals and objectives; nursing procedures are performed and documentation is done at this phase.\n\nEvaluation uses three classic frameworks: structure elements, process elements and outcome elements.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess first, using community data, so goals fit real need.\n2. Set goals with the people, then choose interventions and resources.\n3. Put priorities and a schedule in the operational plan.\n4. Implement and document at once.\n5. Evaluate against the parameters that were written in the planning stage.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: the type of finding changes the action. A deficit needs treatment or rehabilitation, a threat needs prevention and risk removal, a foreseeable crisis needs anticipatory guidance, and wellness potential needs support to keep improving.\n- Order: assessment before planning, goals before interventions, plan before action, evaluation criteria before the action starts. The reason is that you cannot judge success without a pre-set standard.\n- Under limited resources, tackle problems that are life-threatening, spread to others or are easily changed first.\n\n## Key terms and steps\n- Structure: facilities, staff, supplies. Process: how the work is done. Outcome: change in health status.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give a situation and ask which finding it is, or which step of the process an activity belongs to (for example setting evaluation parameters belongs to planning; recording the care given belongs to implementation). Trap: placing documentation only at the end.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Family Nursing Process: Assessment]], [[Family Nursing Process: Planning and Prioritization]], [[Family Nursing Process: Implementation and Evaluation]], [[Community Health Nursing Basics]], [[Management Functions of the Community Health Nurse]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nWhether wellness potential is a fourth category or part of another classification differs by book. Structure, process and outcome are commonly attributed to Donabedian; verify.\n",

"nursing process",
"community diagnosis"

"id": "np1-x-subspecialties",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Community-Based Nursing, Public Health Nursing and CHN Subspecialties",
"body": "## What it is\nSeveral terms sound alike: community health nursing, community-based nursing and public health nursing. The handout separates them and lists the subspecialties of CHN. These definitions are easy exam points.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Community health nursing: synthesis of nursing practice and public health applied to promoting and preserving the health of populations (ANA, 1980). The focus is the community as a whole, with care of individuals, families and groups given within that context.\n- Community-based nursing: a philosophy of care in which care is provided as clients and their families move among various services outside hospitals.\n- Public health nursing: a special field combining the skills of nursing, public health and some phases of social assistance (WHO). It functions within the total public health program to promote health, improve social and physical conditions, and rehabilitate illness and disability.\n- Winslow's public health: the science and art of preventing disease, prolonging life and promoting health and efficiency.\n- Four levels of clientele: individuals, family, population, community.\n- Subspecialties: school nursing, occupational health nursing, community mental health nursing, public health nursing.\n- Public health nurse (handout): a nurse in the local or national health departments or public schools, whatever the title (public health nurse or school nurse); starting salary grade 15.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify the client level (individual, family, population, community) before choosing an approach.\n2. Pick services and settings for that client: school, workplace, home, health center.\n3. Work across services so that the client moves smoothly between them.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a nurse at a factory who monitors worker hazards is occupational health nursing; a nurse who screens pupils is school nursing; a nurse who follows a discharged client at home across clinic and home services is practicing community-based nursing.\n- Focus clue: population-wide prevention and promotion points to public health nursing; care outside hospitals for individuals and families points to community-based nursing.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the definition to the term or the setting to the subspecialty. Trap: treating community-based nursing and public health nursing as identical.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Community Health Nursing Basics]], [[Public Health Nurse Roles and Qualifications]], [[Primary Health Care Basics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe salary grade, the attributions (ANA 1980, WHO) and the exact wording come from the handout; verify them in your textbook.\n",

"id": "np1-x-epi-concepts",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Epidemiology: Multiple Causation, Natural History and Four Approaches",
"body": "## What it is\nEpidemiology studies the occurrence and distribution of health conditions such as disease, death, deformity and disability in human populations. The nurse measures frequency and distribution with vital statistics.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Important concepts: the multiple causation theory (pictured as the wheel, the web and the ecologic triad), the natural history of disease, the levels of prevention of health problems, and the concept of causality and association.\n- Natural history of disease: pre-pathogenesis (susceptibility, before the agent acts) then pathogenesis, which has three substages: pre-symptomatic, discernible lesions, and advanced disease.\n- Four epidemiological approaches: descriptive (distribution and frequency, by person, place and time); analytical (looks for causes and determinants by hypothesis testing); intervention or experimental (tests the effectiveness of new methods of control or improving conditions); evaluation (measures the effectiveness of different health services and programs).\n- Multiple causation means disease usually results from several interacting factors rather than one cause.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Describe the problem first: who, where, when.\n2. Look for factors that explain it and test them.\n3. Choose and try control measures; evaluate whether they work.\n4. Match prevention to the stage of the natural history.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: pre-pathogenesis points to primary prevention (promotion and protection); pre-symptomatic and early lesions point to screening and early treatment; advanced disease points to limiting disability.\n- Order of epidemiologic work: describe, then analyze, then intervene, then evaluate. The reason is that you cannot test causes before the pattern is known, and you cannot evaluate a program that was never tried.\n- Approach recognition: counts and rates by place and time are descriptive; comparing exposed and unexposed to test a cause is analytical; trying a new control method is experimental; judging a program's results is evaluation.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems describe an activity (computing rates, testing a hypothesis, trialing a new bednet) and ask for the approach, or give a stage of illness and ask for the prevention level. Trap: confusing evaluation epidemiology with experimental epidemiology.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Vital Statistics]], [[Levels of Prevention]], [[Epidemiologic Triangle and Patterns of Disease Occurrence]], [[Incidence, Prevalence and Population Pyramid]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nSome books name only three approaches or merge intervention and evaluation; match your textbook.\n",

"id": "np1-x-fhsis-blocks",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "FHSIS Objectives and Building Blocks",
"body": "## What it is\nThe Field Health Services Information System (FHSIS) is the standard record-and-report system of Philippine health facilities. Knowing its building blocks and what each does is a common recall item.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Objectives: give a summary of data on health service delivery; supply data for program monitoring and evaluation; provide a standardized facility-level database that can be used for deeper studies; make sure data are useful and accurate; reduce the recording and reporting burden at the service level.\n- Family Treatment Record: the fundamental building block of FHSIS.\n- Target Client List: the second building block.\n- Reporting forms: the only mechanism through which data are routinely sent from one facility to another; prepared and submitted monthly or quarterly.\n- Output reports: designed so the results are useful for monitoring and management.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Record each contact on the family treatment record at the time of service.\n2. Keep the target client list updated so that defaulters and due clients can be found.\n3. Summarize into the monthly or quarterly report and send it upward.\n4. Use the output reports to plan and manage programs.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if a barangay's coverage looks low, check whether the target client list is up to date before concluding service failure.\n- Order of data flow: individual record, then client list, then report, then output. Each layer is built from the one before, so errors at the base spread upward.\n- The reporting form, not the individual record, is what travels between facilities.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nItems ask which component is the fundamental building block (family treatment record), which is second (target client list), or how data moves between facilities (reporting forms). Trap: swapping the first and second building blocks.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Epidemiology and FHSIS]], [[Vital Statistics]], [[Management Functions of the Community Health Nurse]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nCurrent forms and electronic versions: verify local policy.\n",

"id": "np1-x-epi-law-contraindications",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "EPI Law Principles and Vaccine Contraindications",
"body": "## What it is\nThe handout groups the working principles behind the Expanded Program on Immunization and the lists of false and absolute contraindications. These are favorite exam statements.\n\n## Must-know facts\nPrinciples (EPI law, PD 996 in the handout):\n- It is safe and immunologically effective to give all EPI vaccines on the same day at different body sites.\n- Measles vaccine goes as soon as the child is 9 months old; in an endemic area give as early as 6 months. Protection is about 85 percent when given at 9 months and about 95 percent at one year and older.\n- Do not restart the vaccine schedule if a dose is missed.\n- Giving doses at less than the recommended interval may lessen the antibody response.\n- No extra dose is given to a child or mother who missed a dose of DPT, hepatitis B, OPV or TT; continue where left off.\n- Never reconstitute freeze-dried vaccines with anything except the diluent supplied with them.\n- One syringe, one needle per child.\n- RA 10152 (2011) provides for mandatory basic immunization services for infants and children.\n\nFalse contraindications (vaccinate anyway): malnutrition, low-grade fever, mild respiratory infection, cough, diarrhea, vomiting.\n\nAbsolute contraindications: DPT 2 or 3 to a child who had convulsions or shock within 3 days after the previous dose; vaccines containing whole-cell pertussis in a patient with neurologic disease; live vaccines such as BCG for individuals immunosuppressed by malignant disease.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Screen: look for true contraindications first; safety before coverage.\n2. Do not send away children with false contraindications; each missed visit is a missed protection.\n3. Give the vaccine on the correct date, not earlier than the minimum interval.\n4. Record and arrange the next visit.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a child with cough and mild fever has a false contraindication, so vaccinate; a child with seizures within 3 days after DPT has a true contraindication for the next dose, so withhold it and refer.\n- Order: look for severe reaction to a prior dose, then immunosuppression, then neurologic disease; ignore mild illness.\n- A missed dose is given when seen; shortening intervals reduces the antibody response, so do not give early to catch up.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems list conditions and ask which is a contraindication. Mild illness, malnutrition and diarrhea are the favorite false ones. Trap: restarting a series after a delay.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]], [[EPI Routine Schedule: Minimum Ages, Doses, Routes and Sites]], [[Cold Chain and Vaccine Logistics]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe handout calls the EPI law PD 996; confirm the number. Protection percentages for measles and the list of absolute contraindications should be checked.\n",

"id": "np1-x-epi-schedule-table",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "EPI Routine Schedule: Minimum Ages, Doses, Routes and Sites",
"body": "## What it is\nA compact reference of the handout's routine immunization schedule and the dose, route and site of each vaccine.\n\n## Must-know facts\nMinimum age, number of doses and interval:\n- BCG: at birth, 1 dose.\n- Pentavalent: 6 weeks, 3 doses, 4 weeks apart.\n- OPV: 6 weeks, 3 doses, 4 weeks apart.\n- Hepatitis B: at birth, 1 dose (a later dose 6 weeks after the first in the handout).\n- Measles: 9 months, 1 dose.\n- MMR: 12 months, 1 dose.\n- IPV: 14 weeks, 1 dose.\n\nDose, route and site:\n- BCG 0.05 mL, intradermal, right deltoid region.\n- DPT 0.5 mL, intramuscular, upper outer thigh (vastus lateralis).\n- OPV 2 to 3 drops, oral.\n- Hepatitis B 0.5 mL, intramuscular, upper outer thigh.\n- Measles 0.5 mL, subcutaneous, outer upper arm.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Check the card and age before choosing a vaccine.\n2. Confirm the right route and site (infant IM injections go to the thigh because it has the largest muscle).\n3. Respect minimum ages and intervals.\n4. Record the dose and tell the mother when to return.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a 10-week-old with no vaccines can get BCG, pentavalent 1 and OPV 1 together (different sites), because all are due and safe on the same day.\n- Order: BCG and hepatitis B at birth, then the 6-week visit, then 10 and 14 weeks for the series, then measles at 9 months. The reason is that the first doses wait until maternal protection has waned.\n- A tiny intradermal bleb (BCG) means the correct layer; a deeper injection is wrong.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nStems give an age and ask which vaccine is due, or ask the site and route. Trap: giving measles intramuscularly or BCG subcutaneously.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Expanded Program on Immunization]], [[BCG and Pentavalent Vaccine]], [[EPI Law Principles and Vaccine Contraindications]], [[Vaccine Principles and Nursing Responsibilities]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nThe Philippine schedule changes (vaccines added and intervals revised). The handout lists pentavalent at 6 weeks, and a hepatitis B interval that conflicts with common practice; verify the current schedule with your instructor.\n",

"id": "np1-x-food-safety",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Food Safety: Four Rights and Food Establishment Rating",
"body": "## What it is\nFood safety in the community uses simple rules (the four rights) and inspections of food establishments.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Establishments are appraised on inspection and approval of food sources, containers and transport vehicles; sanitary permit requirements; and up-to-date health certificates for food handlers, cooks and helpers. A DOH order (2006) requires laboratories to use the formalin ether concentration technique for stool exams of food handlers instead of the direct fecal smear.\n- Rating: Class A excellent, Class B very satisfactory, Class C satisfactory.\n- Four rights: right source (buy fresh food, check expiry dates, avoid dented, bulging or broken-seal cans, boil water at a rolling boil for at least 2 minutes); right preparation (keep raw and cooked food apart, buy pasteurized milk and juice, wash raw vegetables, wash hands and utensils, sweep food droppings); right cooking (cook thoroughly to about 70 degrees Celsius in all parts, eat cooked food at once, wash hands before and after eating); right storage (cooked food at room temperature for not more than 2 hours, tight containers, hot above 60 degrees or cold at or below 10 degrees for storage beyond 4 to 5 hours, do not overload the refrigerator with warm food, reheat to at least 70 degrees).\n- Rule: when in doubt, throw it out.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify the likely food source and stop further exposure.\n2. Check food handlers' health certificates and symptoms.\n3. Teach the four rights.\n4. Report suspected outbreaks.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: cooked food left out for hours points to storage failure, raw-cooked contact points to preparation failure; correct the matching right.\n- Order: remove the suspect food first (safety), then teach and inspect.\n- Temperature logic: keep food out of the danger zone between cold and hot limits, which is why it must be kept below 10 or above 60 degrees.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch an error to the right, or identify the rating class. Trap: confusing storage (2 hours at room temperature) with cooking temperature (70 degrees).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Typhoid Fever]], [[Cholera]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nTemperatures, hours and the FECT detail come from the handout; verify them locally.\n",

"id": "np1-x-water-toilet-specs",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "Approved Water and Toilet Facility Levels: Specifications",
"body": "## What it is\nThe handout gives numeric specifications for the three levels of water supply and the three levels of toilet facilities.\n\n## Must-know facts\nWater:\n- Level I (point source): protected well or developed spring with an outlet but no distribution system; serves about 15 to 25 households; farthest user within 250 meters; yield about 40 to 140 liters per minute; for thinly scattered rural houses.\n- Level II (communal faucet or standpost): source or reservoir, piped network and communal faucets; faucet not more than 25 meters from the farthest house; designed for 40 to 80 liters per capita per day; about 100 households; one faucet per 4 to 6 households; for rural areas with clustered houses.\n- Level III (waterworks): individual house connections (examples in the handout are the Manila metropolitan water utilities).\n- Unapproved: open dug wells, unimproved springs, wells that need priming.\n- Disinfect new or repaired water sources. Well sites need prior approval from the Health Secretary or representative.\n\nToilets:\n- Level I: non-water-carriage (pit latrine, reed odorless earth closet) and facilities that need small amounts of water (pour-flush toilet, aqua privy).\n- Level II: water-carriage with septic vault or tank disposal.\n- Level III: water-carriage connected to septic tanks and/or a sewerage system with a treatment plant.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Classify the facility.\n2. Disinfect and test questionable water.\n3. Advise upgrades suited to the settlement.\n4. Teach handwashing.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: if a barangay's clustered houses share a tap, that is Level II; recommend protecting the source and maintaining the faucet.\n- Order: make drinking water safe first, then improve excreta disposal, because contaminated water spreads disease fastest.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch numbers or descriptions to levels. Trap: swapping the 250 m figure (Level I) with the 25 m figure (Level II).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Water Supply and Toilet Facilities]], [[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nHousehold counts and distances are from the handout and differ among sources; verify.\n",

"title": "Environmental Health: Definition and Sanitation Laws",
"body": "## What it is\nEnvironmental health is the part of public health that prevents illness by managing the environment and changing behavior to lower exposure to biological and non-biological agents of disease or injury. Environmental sanitation studies all factors in the physical environment that may harm health, well-being and survival.\n\n## Must-know facts\nLaws listed in the handout:\n- PD 856: Sanitation Code of the Philippines.\n- RA 6969: Toxic Substances and Hazardous and Nuclear Waste Control Act of 1990.\n- RA 8749: Clean Air Act of 1999.\n- RA 9003: Ecological Solid Waste Management Act of 2000.\n- RA 9275: Clean Water Act of 2004.\n\nThe environmental and occupational health office sets policies on approved water supply types, unapproved facilities, safe and potable water access, water quality monitoring, and waterworks and well construction.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess hazards at household and community level.\n2. Remove or reduce the most dangerous hazard first.\n3. Teach behavior change and coordinate with the sanitary inspector.\n4. Report violations and outbreaks.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: a hazard category suggests the law (water, air, waste, toxic).\n- Order: control the exposure that is nearest and most harmful, then upstream causes.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch the law to the topic. Trap: confusing RA 9003 (solid waste) with RA 6969 (toxic and hazardous waste).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Environmental Sanitation]], [[Waste, Food Sanitation and Sanitation Laws]], [[Approved Water and Toilet Facility Levels: Specifications]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nLaw numbers and years: verify.\n",

"id": "np1-x-nutrition-program",
"area": "NP1",
"title": "National Nutrition Program Components",
"body": "## What it is\nThe handout's nutrition program aims to improve quality of life through better nutrition, improved health and more productivity.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Common deficiencies: vitamin A, iron, iodine.\n- Programs and projects: micronutrient supplementation; food fortification; the essential maternal and child health service package; nutrition information, communication and education; home, school and community food production; food assistance; livelihood assistance.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Identify deficiency and risk groups.\n2. Give supplements on schedule.\n3. Teach food production and feeding.\n4. Link families to assistance.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: low income and poor diet point to food assistance and production; knowledge gaps point to education.\n- Order: treat immediate deficiency, then build lasting food security.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nAssign an activity to a component. Trap: confusing fortification (adding nutrients to staple foods) with supplementation (giving tablets or capsules).\n\n## Related topics\n[[Nutrition]], [[Laws Protecting Infant Feeding and Food Fortification]], [[Maternal Health Program: Prenatal Visits, Supplements and Postpartum Care]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nVerify program names and current schedules.\n",

"title": "Philippine Reproductive Health Program: Goals and Elements",
"body": "## What it is\nThe handout describes the national reproductive health program with an overall goal of better quality of life.\n\n## Must-know facts\n- Main objectives: reduce maternal mortality, reduce child mortality, halt and reverse the spread of HIV/AIDS, and increase access to reproductive health information and services.\n- Elements: family planning; maternal and child health and nutrition; prevention and management of reproductive tract infections; adolescent reproductive health; prevention and management of abortion complications; prevention and management of breast and reproductive tract cancers and other gynecologic conditions; education and counseling on sexuality; men's reproductive health and involvement; violence against women and children; prevention and management of infertility and sexual dysfunction.\n\n## What the nurse does (priority order and why)\n1. Assess the client's need and give counseling.\n2. Refer for services beyond the facility.\n3. Protect from violence.\n\n## How to reason about it\n- Cause to finding to action: match the client's problem to the element and offer that service.\n- Order: safety and life-threatening problems first.\n\n## How the exam asks about it\nMatch a service to its element; recall the objectives.\n\n## Related topics\n[[Reproductive Health and Family Planning]], [[VAWC and Violence Against Women and Children]], [[Maternal and Child Health]]\n\n## Check against your textbook\nElement lists vary by source; verify.\n",
